\displaystyle+\limsup_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\left|\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\int_{0}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\mathrm{d}r\,\nu_{\alpha,\,N,\,\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\int_{0}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\mathrm{d}r\,\nu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)\right|\;.

Let us now take the limitsup above when
N\rightarrow\infty
. To
this extent, let us first notice that for all
x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}

and any
r>0
,
\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)

by the positive recurrence of the process (
2.1
) in Proposition
3.3
.
Additionally, since the function
x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}\to\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)

is continuous on the compact set
\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}

by Lemma
4.11
and the probability
\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)

is an increasing function of
N\geq 1
then by Dini’s theorem the
convergence is uniform in
\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
, i.e.

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\left|\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)-\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\right|\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

Additionally, by the weak-convergence of
\nu_{\alpha,\,N,\,\epsilon}

stated in Proposition
4.7

and the continuity of the second probability in Lemma
4.11

one has that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\,\nu_{\alpha,\,N,\,\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\,\nu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)\;.

Hence,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{E}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land M)\nu_{\alpha,\,N,\,\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{E}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land M)\nu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)\;.

As a result, taking the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
,
\alpha\rightarrow 0

in (
4.28
) ensures by Proposition
4.7
,
Lemmas
4.5
and
4.12

that

\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{E}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land M)\nu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{1}{C_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}(x)\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq M)\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x\;.

Moreover, the positive recurrence of the process (
2.1
)
in Proposition
3.3
ensures that
\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}<\infty

almost surely. Therefore, taking the limit above when
M\rightarrow\infty

by the monotone convergence theorem ensures (
2.17
).
∎

5.
Proof of Lemma
4.8

For the sake of notation we shall denote here for
t\geq 0

the vectors
X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t)=(q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t))
,
X^{\epsilon}(t)=(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))

where the process
(X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
satisfies (
4.1
)
and
(X^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
satisfies (
2.1
). Let
us also fix
r>0
and denote for
N>0
, the open set
\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}

defined in (
4.7
).

Notation 5.1
.

From this moment on, different appearances of a constant may stand for different constants unless
otherwise mentioned. For instance, the constant
C(N)
represents a constant depending on the parameter
N>0
, but different appearances of
C(N)
may refer to different quantities.

5.1.
Preliminary results

In this subsection, we state and prove some preliminary results related to the short-time variations of the processes
(X^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
,
(X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
and to the distance between both processes when
\alpha\rightarrow 0
.

Lemma 5.2
.

For any
\beta\in(0,\,1/2)
,

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\sup_{|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\delta,\,t_{1},\,t_{2}\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land r\right]}|X^{\epsilon}(t_{1})-X^{\epsilon}(t_{2})|>\delta^{\beta}\right)\underset{\delta\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0

and

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\alpha\in(0,\,1)}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\sup_{|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\delta,\,t_{1},\,t_{2}\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land r\right]}|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t_{1})-X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t_{2})|>\delta^{\beta}\right)\underset{\delta\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

Lemma 5.3
.

For any
\beta\in(0,\,1/2)
,

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\sup_{t\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land r\right]}|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t)-X^{\epsilon}(t)|>\alpha^{\beta}\right)\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Let us fix
\beta\in(0,1/2)
. Recall that the sample paths of a Brownian motion are, almost-surely,
\lambda
-Hölderian for any
\lambda\in(0,\,1/2)
. Therefore, taking
\lambda\in(\beta,1/2)
ensures the existence of a finite random variable
\mathcal{Z}>0
such that

\sup_{|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\delta,\,t_{1},\,t_{2}\in\left[0,\,r\right]}|B(t_{1})-B(t_{2})|\leq\mathcal{Z}\delta^{\lambda}\;.

Therefore, using Itô’s formula for (
2.1
) and the boundedness of
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
, one has the existence of a constant
C=C(N)>0
independent of
x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
such that,
\mathbb{P}_{x}
almost-surely,
for all
t_{1},\,t_{2}
satisfying
|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\delta
and

t_{1},\,t_{2}\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land r\right]
,

\displaystyle|X^{\epsilon}(t_{1})-X^{\epsilon}(t_{2})|\leq C\delta+C\mathcal{Z}\delta^{\lambda}\leq C\delta^{\lambda}(1+\mathcal{Z})

for
\delta\in[0,1)
. Therefore,

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\sup_{|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\delta,\,t_{1},\,t_{2}\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land r\right]}|X^{\epsilon}(t_{1})-X^{\epsilon}(t_{2})|>\delta^{\beta}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}(C(1+\mathcal{Z})>\delta^{\beta-\lambda})

which vanishes when
\delta
goes to zero since
\lambda>\beta
and
\mathcal{Z}<\infty
almost-surely. The proof of the second estimate in Lemma
5.2
follows similarly by using as well the Hölderian nature of the sample paths of the Brownian motion
(\widetilde{B}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Fix
\beta\in(0,1/2)
. By Lemma
4.3
, there exists a constant
C=C(N)>0
independent of
\alpha
and
x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
such that,
\mathbb{P}_{x}
almost-surely, for all
t\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land r\right]
,

\displaystyle\left|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t)-X^{\epsilon}(t)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{Ct}\left[\alpha\int_{0}^{t}|\nabla U(q^{\epsilon}(u))|\mathrm{d}u+\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\sup_{u\in[0,\,t]}|\widetilde{B}(u)|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{Ct}\left[C^{\prime}\alpha t+\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\sup_{u\in[0,\,t]}|\widetilde{B}(u)|\right]

for some constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(N)
independent of
\alpha
and
x
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\sup_{t\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land r\right]}|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t)-X^{\epsilon}(t)|>\alpha^{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{e}^{Cr}\left[C^{\prime}\alpha^{1-\beta}r+\alpha^{1/2-\beta}\sqrt{2\epsilon}\sup_{u\in[0,\,r]}|\widetilde{B}(u)|\right]>1\right)\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0

since
\beta\in(0,1/2)
.
∎

To conclude this subsection, the following lemma provides a
\mathrm{L}^{2}
control on martingales appearing in Section
5.3
.

Lemma 5.4
.

There exists a constant
C=C(N)
independent of
\alpha
such that for
\lambda>0
,

(5.1)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}\left|\int_{0}^{t\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}}\left\langle q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-s,\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}(u)\right\rangle\right|^{2}\right]\leq C\alpha^{\lambda}

where
s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined in (
2.11
). Also,

(5.2)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}\left|\int_{0}^{t\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}}\left\langle p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-p,\,\mathrm{d}B(u)\right\rangle\right|^{2}\right]\leq C\alpha^{2\lambda}\,.

Proof.

Recall the notation introduced in (
2.9
) and let
x=(q,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
. By Doob’s martingale inequality and Itô’s isometry,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}\left|\int_{0}^{t\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}}\left\langle q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-s,\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}(u)\right\rangle\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{0}^{\alpha^{\lambda}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>u}\left|q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-s\right|^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}u

(5.3)

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{0}^{\alpha^{\lambda}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\big|q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land u)-s\big|^{2}\big]\mathrm{d}u\;.

Additionally, by Itô’s formula and the boundedness of
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
, there exists a constant
C=C(N)>0
independent of
x

and
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that for all
u\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]
,

\big|q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land u)-s\big|^{2}\leq\big|q-s\big|^{2}+Cu+2\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land u}\left\langle q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\rho)-s,\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}(\rho)\right\rangle\;.

Given that the last term in the right-hand side of the
inequality above is a martingale,

\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\big|q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land u)-s\big|^{2}\big]\leq\big|q-s\big|^{2}+Cu\;.

Reinjecting into (
5.3
) ensures the inequality (
5.1
). Similarly, the inequality (
5.2
) relies on the fact that for all
u\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]
,

\big|p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land u)-p\big|^{2}\leq Cu+2\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land u}\left\langle p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\rho)-p,\,\mathrm{d}B(\rho)\right\rangle\;.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}\left|\int_{0}^{t\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}}\left\langle p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-p,\,\mathrm{d}B(u)\right\rangle\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{0}^{\alpha^{\lambda}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\big|p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land u)-p\big|^{2}\big]\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq 4C\int_{0}^{\alpha^{\lambda}}u\,\mathrm{d}u=2C\alpha^{2\lambda}\,,

which concludes the proof of (
5.2
).
∎

5.2.
Neighborhood of
\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}

This subsection aims at providing sharp estimates for the law of the processes
(X^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
in a neighborhood of the boundary
\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
. These results are used in the next subsection to prove Lemma
4.8
.

Lemma 5.5
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
t\in(0,r]
, if
\theta,\epsilon>0
are small enough, then for all measurable set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}(X^{\epsilon}(t)\in A,\,\mathrm{d}(X^{\epsilon}(t),\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})\leq\theta,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>t)\leq C\,|A|\,,

where
|A|
is the Euclidean volume of
A
.

Corollary 5.6
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
t\in(0,r]
, if
\eta,\,\eta^{\prime},\,\epsilon>0
are small enough, then

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\big|q^{\epsilon}(t)-s\big|\in(\epsilon-\eta,\epsilon+\eta),\,\big|p^{\epsilon}(t)\big|\leq\eta^{\prime},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>t\right)\leq C\eta\,\eta^{\prime}.

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

Friedman’s uniqueness result
[
14
, Theorem 5.2.1]

ensures that the trajectories of
(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))_{0\leq t\leq\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}}

do not depend on the values of the coefficients in (
2.1
) outside of the bounded domain
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
. Since the probability above involves the law of the vector
(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))
under the event
t<\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}
then there is no loss of generality in considering the law of
(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))
where the coefficients in (
2.1
) are modified in
\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
so that they are bounded and globally Lipschitz continuous in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. With this setting in mind we can then apply the result
[
22
, Theorem 2.1]

ensuring the existence of a Gaussian upper-bound given by

(5.4)

f_{t}(q,\,p,\,q^{\prime},\,p^{\prime}):=\frac{c_{1}}{t^{2d}}\mathrm{exp}\big(-c_{2}\left[\frac{|p-p^{\prime}|^{2}}{4t}+3\frac{|q^{\prime}-q-(p+p^{\prime})t/2|^{2}}{t^{3}}\right]\big)\;,

where
c_{1},\,c_{2}>0
are constants independent of
t
. Let us now prove that for all
(q,p),\,(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
satisfying

(q,p)\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{d}((q^{\prime},p^{\prime}),\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})\leq\theta

where
\theta
is sufficiently small, then
f_{t}(q,\,p,\,q^{\prime},\,p^{\prime})
admits an upper-bound independent of
t
and
(q,p),\,(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})
. This would allow to conclude the proof of Lemma
5.5
.

Given that
(q,p)\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
, it satisfies in particular
|q-m|\leq\epsilon
and
|p|\leq\epsilon
. Assume now that
|p^{\prime}|\geq 2\epsilon
then necessarily
|p-p^{\prime}|\geq\epsilon
. As a result,

f_{t}(q,\,p,\,q^{\prime},\,p^{\prime})\leq\frac{c_{1}}{t^{2d}}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{2}\epsilon^{2}/4t}

which can be bounded by a constant
C>0
only depending on
\epsilon
and
c_{1},\,c_{2}
. Consider now the case
|p^{\prime}|\leq 2\epsilon
. Since
\mathrm{d}((q^{\prime},p^{\prime}),\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})\leq\theta
, then if
\theta
is small enough either
|q^{\prime}|\geq N/2
or
|q^{\prime}-s|\leq 2\epsilon
. Assume for instance that
|q^{\prime}-s|\leq 2\epsilon
, then by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle|q^{\prime}-q-(p+p^{\prime})t/2|

\displaystyle\geq|q^{\prime}-q|-|(p+p^{\prime})t/2|

\displaystyle\geq|m-s|-|q^{\prime}-s|-|q-m|-3\epsilon r/2

\displaystyle\geq|m-s|/2

when
\epsilon
is small enough since
|q-m|\leq\epsilon
. The reasoning is similar if
|q^{\prime}|\geq N/2
. As a result, one is able to deduce in both cases an upper-bound of
f_{t}(q,\,p,\,q^{\prime},\,p^{\prime})
which is independent of
t
and
(q,p),\,(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})
, hence the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
5.6
.

Let us take
\theta,\,\epsilon>0
small enough as assumed in Lemma
5.5
. It is easy to see that under the event

\big\{\big|q^{\epsilon}(t)-s\big|\in(\epsilon-\eta,\epsilon+\eta),\,\big|p^{\epsilon}(t)\big|\leq\eta^{\prime}\big\}\,,

if
\eta,\,\eta^{\prime}
are small enough then
\mathrm{d}(X^{\epsilon}(t),\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})\leq\theta
. The proof then follows from Lemma
5.5
and Weyl’s tube formula
[
37
]
.
∎

The following lemma is crucial to control the velocity of the process (
2.1
) entering the set
\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
, which is needed in the proof of Lemma
4.8
in the next subsection. It follows the outline of the proof in
[
28
, Proposition 2.7]
.

Lemma 5.7
.

For
\epsilon
small enough
and
N
large enough,

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\delta,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r\right)\underset{\delta\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

Proof.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
. Given the definition of
\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
in (
4.7
), either
X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}
or
X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathrm{B}(0,N)
. Therefore, let us start by considering the first case and we prove that for any
\nu>0
, if
\delta
is small enough then

(5.5)

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon},\,|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\delta,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r\right)\leq\nu\;.

Let
\eta\in(0,\,1/2)
and let

\mathcal{Z}:=\sup_{0\leq t<t^{\prime}\leq r}\frac{|B(t)-B(t^{\prime})|}{|t-t^{\prime}|^{\eta}},

then
\mathcal{Z}<\infty
, almost-surely, since the Brownian motion

(B(t))_{t\geq 0}
is
\eta
-Hölderian.

Let
J_{\delta}=\lfloor 1/\delta\rfloor
and let
r_{k}=r\frac{k}{J_{\delta}}
for
0\leq k\leq J_{\delta}
. We take
M>0
large enough such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Z}>M)\leq\nu/2
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon},\,|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\delta,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon},\,|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\delta,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r,\,\mathcal{Z}\leq M\right)+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Z}>M)

(5.6)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{J_{\delta}-1}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in(r_{k},\,r_{k+1}],\,X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon},\,|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\delta,\,\mathcal{Z}\leq M\right)+\frac{\nu}{2}\;.

Moreover, by (
2.1
), for all
u\in[r_{k},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}]
,

p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})-p^{\epsilon}(u)=\int_{u}^{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}}\left[-\nabla U(q^{\epsilon}(\rho))-\gamma p^{\epsilon}(\rho)\right]\mathrm{d}\rho+\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\left(B(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})-B(u)\right)\;.

Under the event
\{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in(r_{k},\,r_{k+1}],\,\mathcal{Z}\leq M\}
, using the boundedness of
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
there exists a constant
C=C(N)\geq 1
such that

(5.7)

\big|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})-p^{\epsilon}(u)\big|\leq\frac{C}{J_{\delta}}+\frac{C}{J_{\delta}^{\eta}}\leq\frac{2C}{J_{\delta}^{\eta}}\;.

In particular, under the event
\{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in(r_{k},\,r_{k+1}],\,|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\delta,\,\mathcal{Z}\leq M\}
, one has by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\big|p^{\epsilon}(r_{k})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})-p^{\epsilon}(r_{k})\big|+\big|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\big|

(5.8)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2C}{J_{\delta}^{\eta}}+\frac{1}{J_{\delta}}\leq\frac{3C}{J_{\delta}^{\eta}}\,,

since
\delta\leq 1/J_{\delta}
, by definition of
J_{\delta}
. Moreover, under the event
\{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in(r_{k},\,r_{k+1}],\,|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\delta,\,\mathcal{Z}\leq M\}
, the triangle inequality also yields by (
5.7
),

\displaystyle\big|q^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})-q^{\epsilon}(r_{k})\big|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{r_{k}}^{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}}p^{\epsilon}(u)\mathrm{d}u\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}}+\int_{r_{k}}^{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}}\big|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\big|\mathrm{d}u

(5.9)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}}+\frac{1}{J_{\delta}^{2}}\leq\frac{3C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}}\,.

Furthermore, on the event
\{X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}\}
,

(5.10)

\big|q^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})-s\big|^{2}+\big|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\big|^{2}=\epsilon^{2}\;.

Therefore, on the event
\{X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon},\,\big|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\big|\leq\delta\}
,

\sqrt{\epsilon^{2}-\frac{1}{J^{2}_{\delta}}}\leq\big|q^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})-s\big|\leq\epsilon\;.

Combining the inequality above with (
5.9
) one obtains

\sqrt{\epsilon^{2}-\frac{1}{J^{2}_{\delta}}}-\frac{3C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}}\leq\big|q^{\epsilon}(r_{k})-s\big|\leq\epsilon+\frac{3C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}}\;.

Taking
\delta
sufficiently small and applying the asymptotic expansion of
\sqrt{1-x}
when
x\rightarrow 0
, one obtains the existence of a constant
C=C(N,\epsilon)>0
which is independent of
\delta
, such that

\epsilon-\frac{C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}}\leq\big|q^{\epsilon}(r_{k})-s\big|\leq\epsilon+\frac{C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}}\;.

All in all, combining the inequality above with (
5.8
) and taking a constant
C>0
large enough, one obtains for all
0\leq k\leq J_{\delta}-1
and
x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in(r_{k},\,r_{k+1}],\,X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon},\,|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\delta,\,\mathcal{Z}\leq M\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in(r_{k},\,r_{k+1}],\,\big|q^{\epsilon}(r_{k})-s\big|\in\big[\epsilon-\frac{C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}},\,\epsilon+\frac{C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}}\big],\,\big|p^{\epsilon}(r_{k})\big|\leq\frac{C}{J^{\eta}_{\delta}}\right)\;.

In particular, for
k=0
, since the initial condition
x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
, it is easy to see that, taking
\epsilon
and
\delta
sufficiently small, ensures that the probability above vanishes. Therefore, it is sufficient to consider the case
1\leq k\leq J_{\delta}-1
. Moreover, by Corollary
5.6
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
independent of
k,\delta
and
x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in(r_{k},\,r_{k+1}],\,\big|q^{\epsilon}(r_{k})-s\big|\in\big[\epsilon-\frac{C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}},\,\epsilon+\frac{C}{J_{\delta}^{1+\eta}}\big],\,\big|p^{\epsilon}(r_{k})\big|\leq\frac{C}{J^{\eta}_{\delta}}\right)\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{J_{\delta}^{1+2\eta}}\,.

Reinjecting in (
5.6
) and summing over
1\leq k\leq J_{\delta}-1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon},\,|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\delta,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r\right)\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{J_{\delta}^{2\eta}}+\frac{\nu}{2}\;.

Taking
\delta
sufficiently small concludes the proof. It remains however to also show that

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\partial\mathrm{B}(0,\,N),\,|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\delta,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r\right)\underset{\delta\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

Nonetheless, a similar proof also applies in this case by replacing the equality (
5.10
) by the appropriate formula on
\partial\mathrm{B}(0,\,N)
, hence the proof of this lemma.
∎

5.3.
Proof of Lemma
4.8

The lemma below investigates the probability of not entering the set
\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
in a short time interval when the process (
4.1
) is close to its boundary. We then conclude this section with the proof of Lemma
4.8
. Throughout this subsection, we will regularly use the notation (
2.9
).

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\lambda\in(0,1/2)
and define

(5.11)

\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}:=\{x=(q,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}:|p|>\alpha^{\lambda/4}/2,\,\mathrm{d}(x,\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})\in(0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]\}\;.

Then,

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\;\epsilon}>\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

Proof.

Let
x=(q,p)\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}
.
Given the definition of
\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
in (
4.7
),
one has that

\mathrm{d}(x,\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})=\mathrm{d}(x,\,\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})\land\mathrm{d}(x,\,\partial\mathrm{B}(0,\,N))\;.

Let us first assume that
\mathrm{d}(x,\,\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})\leq\alpha^{\lambda}
. Then, there exists a constant
c>0
independent of
\alpha
such that for all
\alpha
small enough,

|x-(s,0)|^{2}\leq(\epsilon+\alpha^{\lambda})^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}+c\alpha^{\lambda}\;.

As a result,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t)-(s,0)|^{2}-|x-(s,0)|^{2}\geq-c\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\;.

Furthermore, by Itô’s formula applied to (
4.1
), almost-surely
for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t)-(s,0)|^{2}-|x-(s,0)|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{t}\left[\left\langle q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-s,\,p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-\alpha\nabla U(q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u))\right\rangle+\left\langle p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u),\,-\gamma p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-\nabla U(q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u))\right\rangle\right]\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+\,d(\gamma+\alpha)\epsilon t+\,2\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-s,\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}(u)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\,2\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-p,\,\mathrm{d}B(u)\right\rangle+2\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\left\langle p,\,B(t)\right\rangle\;.

Consider now the event

\mathcal{G}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon}:=\left\{2\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\sup_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-s,\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}(u)\right\rangle+2\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\sup_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-p,\,\mathrm{d}B(u)\right\rangle\leq 4\alpha^{4\lambda/5}\right\}\;,

then under the event
\{\mathcal{G}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon},\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>\alpha^{\lambda}\}

there exists a constant
C=C(N)>0
independent of
\alpha

such that for all
t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]
and
\alpha
small enough,

\displaystyle|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t)-(s,0)|^{2}-|x-(s,0)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha^{\lambda}+4\alpha^{4\lambda/5}+2\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\left\langle p,\,B(t)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\alpha^{4\lambda/5}+2\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\left\langle p,\,B(t)\right\rangle

where
C^{\prime}=C+4>0
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(C^{\prime}\alpha^{4\lambda/5}+2\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\inf_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}\left\langle p,\,B(t)\right\rangle\geq-c\alpha^{\lambda}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>\alpha^{\lambda},\,\mathcal{G}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon}^{c}\right)\;.

Given that
(\left\langle p,\,B(t)\right\rangle/|p|)_{t\geq 0}
is a one-dimensional Brownian motion, letting
G\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, one has that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(2\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\inf_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}\left\langle p,\,B(t)\right\rangle\geq-C^{\prime}\alpha^{4\lambda/5}-c\alpha^{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(2\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\,\alpha^{\lambda/2}|G|<C^{\prime}\frac{\alpha^{4\lambda/5}}{|p|}+c\,\frac{\alpha^{\lambda}}{|p|}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(2\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\,|G|<2C^{\prime}\,\alpha^{\lambda/20}+2c\,\alpha^{\lambda/4}\right)\;,

since
|p|>\alpha^{\lambda/4}/2
, which therefore converges to
0

when
\alpha
goes to zero.

It remains to show that

(5.12)

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>\alpha^{\lambda},\,\mathcal{G}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon}^{c}\right)\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

Notice that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>\alpha^{\lambda},\,\mathcal{G}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon}^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\sup_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}\int_{0}^{t\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}}\left\langle q^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-s,\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}(u)\right\rangle>\alpha^{4\lambda/5}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\sup_{t\in[0,\,\alpha^{\lambda}]}\int_{0}^{t\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}}\left\langle p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(u)-p,\,\mathrm{d}B(u)\right\rangle>\alpha^{4\lambda/5}\right)\;.

Using the Markov inequality for both probabilities in the right-hand side above, along with Lemma
5.4
, one deduces the existence of a constant
C>0
independent of
\alpha
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>\alpha^{\lambda},\,\mathcal{G}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon}^{c}\right)\leq C\frac{\alpha^{1+\lambda}}{\alpha^{8\lambda/5}}+C\frac{\alpha^{2\lambda}}{\alpha^{8\lambda/5}}\leq C\,\alpha^{1-3\lambda/5}+C\,\alpha^{2\lambda/5}\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\,,

since
\lambda\in(0,1/2)
. To conclude the proof of this lemma, it remains to investigate the case
\mathrm{d}(x,\,\partial\mathrm{B}(0,N))\leq\alpha^{\lambda}
. However, the proof applies similarly to this case as the argument developed previously mainly depends on the ball-shaped
boundary which is valid for both boundaries
\partial\mathrm{B}(0,\,N)

and
\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.8
.

One has for any
x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
,

\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r)-\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)=\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r)-\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\leq r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\;.

Therefore, it is enough to show that

(5.13)

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r)\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0,\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\leq r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

Let us first show the convergence of the first supremum. Let us take

\lambda\in(0,\,1/2)
, then

(5.14)

\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r)\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in[r-\alpha^{\lambda},\,r])+\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda})\;.

Let us first show that

(5.15)

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in[r-\alpha^{\lambda},\,r])\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

For any
\beta\in(0,\,\lambda/2)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\in[r-\alpha^{\lambda},\,r])

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}(\mathrm{d}(X_{r-\alpha^{\lambda}}^{\epsilon},\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})\leq\alpha^{\beta},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r-\alpha^{\lambda})

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\sup_{|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\alpha^{\lambda},\,t_{1},\,t_{2}\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land r\right]}|X^{\epsilon}(t_{1})-X^{\epsilon}(t_{2})|>\alpha^{\beta}\right)\;.

Using Lemma
5.5
along with Weyl’s tube formula
[
37
]
one obtains the existence of constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
independent of
\alpha
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\mathrm{d}(X^{\epsilon}({r-\alpha^{\lambda}}),\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})\leq\alpha^{\beta},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r-\alpha^{\lambda})\leq C_{1}\int_{x:\mathrm{d}(x,\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})\leq\alpha^{\beta}}\mathrm{d}x\leq C_{1}C_{2}\,\alpha^{\beta}\;.

Additionally, by Lemma
5.2
,

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\sup_{|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\alpha^{\lambda},\,t_{1},\,t_{2}\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land r\right]}|X^{\epsilon}(t_{1})-X^{\epsilon}(t_{2})|>\alpha^{\beta}\right)\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;,

hence the proof of (
5.15
).

We consider now the second term in (
5.14
).
One has that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda})

(5.16)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\big|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\big|\leq\alpha^{\lambda/4},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\big|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\big|>\alpha^{\lambda/4},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\;.

It follows from Lemma
5.7
that for

\epsilon\in(0,\,1)
small enough,

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|\leq\alpha^{\lambda/4},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

It remains to consider the second probability in (
5.16
).
One has

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(|p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|>\alpha^{\lambda/4},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})-X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|>\alpha^{\lambda},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda}\right)

(5.17)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(|p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|>\alpha^{\lambda/4}/2,\,\mathrm{d}(X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}),\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})\leq\alpha^{\lambda},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\;.

Notice that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})-X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|>\alpha^{\lambda},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\sup_{t\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land r\right]}|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(t)-X^{\epsilon}(t)|\geq\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\;.

Therefore, since
\lambda\in(0,\,1/2)
, it follows from Lemma
5.3

that

\sup_{x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(|X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})-X^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|>\alpha^{\lambda},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;,

Consider now the last term in the right-hand side of (
5.17
).
One has by the strong Markov property at the stopping time
\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(|p^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})|>\alpha^{\lambda/4}/2,\,\mathrm{d}(X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}),\,\partial\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon})\leq\alpha^{\lambda},\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>r,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq r-\alpha^{\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{X^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}>\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\right]

where
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}
is defined in (
5.11
). Therefore, the probability above is bounded by

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\;\epsilon}>\alpha^{\lambda}\right)\,,

which vanishes when
\alpha\rightarrow 0
by Lemma
5.8
, hence the proof.
∎

6.
Proof of Proposition
2.10

Recall the notation introduced in (
2.9
). For
\delta>0
, let us define the ball

Q_{\delta}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}:|q|<\delta^{3},\,|p|<\delta\}\;.

Let us take in this section
\epsilon>0
small enough such that for
all
x\in Q_{1}
,

(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q,\,-\sqrt{\epsilon}p)\in\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{c}\;.

We define
u_{\epsilon}
as follows

(6.1)

u_{\epsilon}:x\in Q_{1}\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q,\,-\sqrt{\epsilon}p)}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\right]\;.

The proof of Proposition
2.10
is based on the lemmas
below.

Lemma 6.1
.

We have that

\liminf_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}u_{\epsilon}(0,\,0)>0\;.

Lemma 6.2

(Hölder regularity)
.

There exist constants
c_{1},\,c_{2},\,\lambda>0
and
R^{\prime}\in(0,1)

independent of
\epsilon
such that for all
\epsilon
small enough,
for all
x,\,x^{\prime}\in Q_{R^{\prime}}
,

|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})|\leq(c_{1}+c_{2}u_{\epsilon}(x))|x^{\prime}-x|^{\lambda}\;.

These lemmas are proven in the rest of this section. Using these lemmas
let us now prove Proposition
2.10
.

Proof.

Let
\beta>1/2
and let
\epsilon
be small enough such that
\epsilon^{\beta-1/2}\leq R^{\prime 3}

where
R^{\prime}
is defined in Lemma
6.2
.
Therefore, by Lemma
6.2
, for all
|q|,\,|p|\leq\epsilon^{\beta-1/2}
,

|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}(0,\,0)|\leq(c_{1}+c_{2}u_{\epsilon}(0,\,0))\epsilon^{\lambda(\beta-1/2)}

for some constants
c_{1},\,c_{2},\,\lambda>0
independent of
\epsilon
.
By Lemma
6.1
, we deduce that there exists
c_{3}>0

independent of
\epsilon
such that for
\epsilon
small enough,

|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}(0,\,0)|\leq c_{3}u_{\epsilon}(0,\,0)\epsilon^{\lambda(\beta-1/2)}

Therefore, for all
|q|,\,|p|\leq\epsilon^{\beta-1/2}
,

u_{\epsilon}(q,\,p)=u_{\epsilon}(0,\,0)(1+\underset{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}{O}(\epsilon^{\delta}))\;,

where
\delta=\lambda(\beta-1/2)>0
. The proof of Proposition
2.10

then follows from the expression of
u_{\epsilon}
in (
6.1
).
∎

Let us now prove Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

Since
u_{\epsilon}\geq 0
, let us assume that

\liminf_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}u_{\epsilon}(0,\,0)=0\;.

Then one can build a sequence
(\epsilon_{n})_{n\geq 0}
such that

\epsilon_{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
and

u_{\epsilon_{n}}(0,\,0)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
.
By definition of
u_{\epsilon_{n}}
, this ensures that under
\mathbb{P}_{(m,\,0)}
,

\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon_{n}}}^{\epsilon_{n}}\overset{\mathrm{L}^{1}}{\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}}0\;.

Up to taking a subsequence of
(\epsilon_{n})_{n\geq 0}
one can assume
that
\mathbb{P}_{(m,\,0)}
almost-surely,

\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon_{n}}}^{\epsilon_{n}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

Let
K
be a ball of radius
\theta>0
, independent of
n
, centered
around
(m,\,0)
and let
\tau_{K^{c}}^{\epsilon_{n}}
be the first
exit time of (
2.1
) from
K
then there exists a constant

C>0
independent of
n\geq 0
such that,
\mathbb{P}_{(m,\,0)}

almost-surely,

0<\theta=\left|\left(q^{\epsilon_{n}}(\tau_{K^{c}}^{\epsilon_{n}}),\,p^{\epsilon_{n}}(\tau_{K^{c}}^{\epsilon_{n}})\right)-(m,\,0)\right|\leq C\tau_{K^{c}}^{\epsilon_{n}}+\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon_{n}}\sup_{r\in[0,\,\tau_{K^{c}}^{\epsilon_{n}}]}|B(r)|\;.

Furthermore, up to taking
\theta
small enough one has by the continuity
of the trajectories of (
2.1
) that almost-surely
\tau_{K^{c}}^{\epsilon_{n}}<\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon_{n}}}^{\epsilon_{n}}
.
Reinjecting into the inequality above ensures that

0<\theta\leq C\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon_{n}}}^{\epsilon_{n}}+\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon_{n}}\sup_{r\in[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon_{n}}}^{\epsilon_{n}}]}|B_{r}|\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\;,

since
\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon_{n}}}^{\epsilon_{n}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
,
which is a clear contradiction. Hence the proof.
∎

In order to prove Lemma
6.2
we shall
first consider the processes (
2.1
) and (
4.1
)
when the force
-\nabla U
is smoothened. Namely, for
n\geq 1
, for

q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let us define

(6.2)

F_{n}(q)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla U(q-q^{\prime})\phi_{n}(q^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}\;,

where
\phi_{n}(q^{\prime})=n^{d}\phi(nq^{\prime})
with
\phi
a smooth compactly
supported function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
integrating to
1
. Then,

F_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\mathbb{R}^{d})
and for all compact
set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(6.3)

\sup_{q\in K}|F_{n}(q)+\nabla U(q)|\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\;,

since
U\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\mathbb{R})
.

Let us also denote by
(X^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t)=(q^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t)))_{t\geq 0}

the process satisfying (
4.1
) where
-\nabla U
is
replaced by the function
F_{n}
. Similarly, denote by
(X^{n,\,\epsilon}(t)=(q^{n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,p^{n,\,\epsilon}(t)))_{t\geq 0}

the process satisfying (
2.1
) where
-\nabla U
is replaced
by the function
F_{n}
. Additionally, for a given set
\mathcal{C}
,
we denote by
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}
(resp.

\tau_{\mathcal{C}}^{n,\,\epsilon}
) the first hitting time of
\mathcal{C}

for the process
(X^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
(resp.

(X^{n,\,\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
).

In order to prove Lemma
6.2
we shall
require the following lemma which proof is provided below.

Lemma 6.3
.

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d},

\lim_{N,\,M\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}\land M\right]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\right]\;,

where
\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}
is defined in (
4.7
).

Proof.

For all
\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,M>0
, by the Fubini permutation,

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}\land M\right]=\int_{0}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}>r)\mathrm{d}r\;.

Therefore, the proof of (
4.26
) in Lemma
4.12

which remains identical when replacing
-\nabla U
by
F_{n}
, guarantees
by the dominated convergence theorem that

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}\land M\right]\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\int_{0}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{n,\,\epsilon}>r)\mathrm{d}r\;.

Let us now look at the convergence of
\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{n,\,\epsilon}>r)

when
n\rightarrow\infty
. In order to do that let us apply Grönwall’s
lemma which provides the existence of a constant
C>0
depending
on
N
but independent of
n
such that, almost surely, for all

t\in\left[0,\,\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{n,\,\epsilon}\land\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land M\right]
,

\left|X^{n,\,\epsilon}(t)-X^{\epsilon}(t)\right|\leq M\sup_{(q,p)\in\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}^{c}}|F_{n}(q)+\nabla U(q)|\mathrm{e}^{CM}\;,

which goes to zero when
n\rightarrow\infty
by (
6.3
)
since
\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}^{c}
is a compact set of
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
Consequently, following the proof of (
4.26
)
in Lemma
4.12
, one also obtains the
following convergence

\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{n,\,\epsilon}>r)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\;.

It remains to consider the limit of
\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)

when
N\rightarrow\infty
, which follows immediately from the application
of the monotone convergence theorem since
\cup_{N\geq 1}\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}=\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}
.
Therefore,

\int_{0}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\mathrm{d}r\underset{N,\,M\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\mathrm{d}r=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\right]\;,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Let us conclude this section with the proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}
,
let

v_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}\land M\right]\;.

Following the same computation done in
Step 4
of the proof
of Proposition
4.7
, one easily
shows that

v_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{X^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(r)}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}\leq M)\mathrm{d}r\right]\;.

Therefore, it follows from
[
14
, Theorem 6.5.1]
that
v_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}

is a classical solution of the following equation, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}
,

\displaystyle\langle p,\,\nabla_{q}v_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)\rangle+\langle F_{n}(q)-\gamma p,\,\nabla_{p}v_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)\rangle

(6.4)

\displaystyle+\gamma\epsilon\Delta_{p}v_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)+\alpha\epsilon\Delta_{q}v_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)=-\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}\leq M)\;.

Let us now define for all
x=(q,p)\in Q_{1}
,

u_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x):=v_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q,\,-\sqrt{\epsilon}p)\;.

It follows from (
6.4
) that
u_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}

satisfies the following equation

\displaystyle-\langle p,\,\nabla_{q}u_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)\rangle-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\langle F_{n}(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q),\,\nabla_{p}u_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)\rangle-\gamma\langle p,\,\nabla_{p}u_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle+\gamma\Delta_{p}u_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)+\alpha\Delta_{q}u_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)=-\mathbb{P}_{(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q,\,-\sqrt{\epsilon}p)}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}\leq M)\;.

For all
x\in Q_{1}
, let us define its limit

u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x):=\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}u_{M,\,\alpha,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)\;,

which exists by Lemma
6.3
.
Then, taking the limit of (
6.4
) when

\alpha\rightarrow 0
and using (
4.26
) in
Lemma
4.12
, one has that
u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}

is a weak solution in
Q_{1}
, in the sense of distributions, of

\displaystyle-\langle p,\,\nabla_{q}u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)\rangle-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\langle F_{n}(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q),\,\nabla_{p}u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)\rangle-\gamma\langle p,\,\nabla_{p}u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle+\gamma\Delta_{p}u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)=-\mathbb{P}_{(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q,\,-\sqrt{\epsilon}p)}(\tau_{\mathcal{O}_{N,\,\epsilon}}^{n,\,\epsilon}\leq M)\;.

Let

(6.5)

A_{n,\,\epsilon}\geq\sup_{x\in Q_{1}}\left|\gamma p+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}F_{n}(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q)\right|\;.

Applying the Harnack inequality in
[
18
, Theorem 4]
to the
function
u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}
in
Q_{1}
, one has the existence
of constants
R\in(0,\,1)
,
C>0
only depending on
A_{n,\,\epsilon}

and
\gamma
such that

(6.6)

\sup_{x\in Q_{R}}u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)\leq C\left(\inf_{x\in Q_{R}}u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)+1\right)\;.

Additionally, it follows from
[
18
, Theorem 3]
that there exist
constants
\lambda>0
and
C^{\prime}>0
only depending on the dimension

d
and on
\gamma,\,A_{n,\,\epsilon}
and
R
such that for all

x,\,x^{\prime}\in Q_{R/2}
,

(6.7)

|u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x^{\prime})-u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)|\leq C^{\prime}\left(1+\sqrt{\int_{Q_{R}}|u_{M,\,n,\,N,\,\epsilon}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x}\right)|x^{\prime}-x|^{\lambda}\;.

We would like to take the limit when
n,\,M,\,N\rightarrow\infty

in (
6.6
) and (
6.7
) using Lemma
6.3

and consider the behaviour when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. In this regard
we want to show that the constants
R,\,C,\,\lambda,\,C^{\prime}
are independent
of
n,\,\epsilon
. This amounts to proving that
A_{n,\,\epsilon}

can be bounded from above by a constant independent of
n,\,\epsilon
.

In order to do that, notice that since
m
is a critical point of

U
, one has for all
x=(q,\,p)\in Q_{1}
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\left|F_{n}(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q)\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\left|F_{n}(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q)+\nabla U(m)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla U(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q+q^{\prime})-\nabla U(m)\right|n^{d}\phi(nq^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\nabla U(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q+q^{\prime}/n)-\nabla U(m)\right|\phi(q^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}\;.

Besides, since
\phi
is compactly supported in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

and
U
is
C^{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists a constant

c>0
independent of
\epsilon,n
such that for all
|q|\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\left|F_{n}(m+\sqrt{\epsilon}q)\right|

\displaystyle\leq c+\frac{c}{n\sqrt{\epsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|q^{\prime}\right|\phi(q^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}\;.

Therefore, by taking
n
large enough, namely
n\geq 1/\sqrt{\epsilon}
,
one can has that
A_{n,\,\epsilon}
defined in (
6.5
)
can be taken independently of
n
and
\epsilon
for
n\geq 1/\sqrt{\epsilon}

which allows us by Lemma
6.3

to take the limit when
n\rightarrow\infty
and
N,\,M\rightarrow\infty

in (
6.6
) and (
6.7
). Consequently, by
definition of
u_{\epsilon}
in (
6.1
), one obtains

(6.8)

\sup_{x\in Q_{R}}u_{\epsilon}(x)\leq C\left(\inf_{x\in Q_{R}}u_{\epsilon}(x)+1\right)\;.

Moreover, for all
x,\,x^{\prime}\in Q_{R/2}
,

(6.9)

|u_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})-u_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq C^{\prime}\left(1+\sqrt{\int_{Q_{R}}|u_{\epsilon}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x}\right)|x^{\prime}-x|^{\alpha}\;.

Furthermore, we deduce from (
6.8
) that for all
x\in Q_{R}
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\int_{Q_{R}}|u_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})|^{2}\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2C^{2}u_{\epsilon}(x)^{2}|Q_{R}|+2C^{2}|Q_{R}|}

\displaystyle\leq u_{\epsilon}(x)\sqrt{2C^{2}|Q_{R}|}+\sqrt{2C^{2}|Q_{R}|}\;.

Therefore, for all
x,\,x^{\prime}\in Q_{R/2}
,

\displaystyle|u_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})-u_{\epsilon}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\left(1+\sqrt{2C^{2}|Q_{R}|}+u_{\epsilon}(x)\sqrt{2C^{2}|Q_{R}|}\right)|x^{\prime}-x|^{\alpha}\;,

which concludes the proof with
R^{\prime}=R/2
.
∎

7.
Proof of Proposition
2.11

The proof of Proposition
2.11
relies on the important
result below which provides sharp estimates on the function
h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}
. Consider
the set

(7.1)

\mathcal{W}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}:V(x)<V(\sigma,0)\}\;.

We denote by
\mathcal{W}_{m}
(resp.
\mathcal{W}_{s}
) the connected
component of
\mathcal{W}
containing the local minimum
(m,\,0)

(resp.
(s,\,0)
) of
V
.

Proposition 7.1
.

There exist constants
C,\,\beta>0
independent
of
\epsilon
such that for all
\epsilon
small enough,

•

for all
x\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
,

h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\geq 1-\frac{C}{\epsilon^{\beta}}\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{V(x)-V(\sigma,\,0)}{\epsilon}\right)\;.

•

for all
x\in\mathcal{W}_{s}
,

h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon^{\beta}}\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{V(x)-V(\sigma,\,0)}{\epsilon}\right)\;.

The proof of Proposition
7.1
is based on the two propositions stated below. Define the process
(\widetilde{q}^{\epsilon}(t),\,\widetilde{p}^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}

(7.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{d}\widetilde{q}^{\epsilon}(t)=-\widetilde{p}^{\epsilon}(t)\mathrm{d}t,\\
&\mathrm{d}\widetilde{p}^{\epsilon}(t)=\nabla U(\widetilde{q}^{\epsilon}(t))\mathrm{d}t+\gamma\widetilde{p}^{\epsilon}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\mathrm{d}B(t)\;.\end{aligned}\right.

Let

(7.3)

\zeta^{\epsilon}:=\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}}^{\epsilon}\land\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\land\tau_{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon},\quad\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon}:=\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{W}}^{\epsilon}\land\widetilde{\tau}_{\mathcal{M_{\epsilon}}}^{\epsilon}\land\widetilde{\tau}_{\mathcal{S_{\epsilon}}}^{\epsilon}\;,

where
\widetilde{\tau}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\epsilon}
(resp.
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}^{\epsilon}
)
is the first hitting time of the set
\mathcal{C}
for the process (
7.2
)
(resp. (
2.1
)).

Proposition 7.2
.

For all
x=(q,p)\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
,

h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\geq 1-\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{V(x)-V(\sigma,\,0)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathbb{E}_{(q,\,-p)}\left[\mathrm{exp}(d\gamma\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon})\right]\;.

For all
x\in\mathcal{W}_{s}
,

h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\leq\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{V(x)-V(\sigma,\,0)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathbb{E}_{(q,\,-p)}\left[\mathrm{exp}(d\gamma\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon})\right]\;.

Proposition 7.3
.

There exist
C,\,\beta>0

independent of
\epsilon
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{W}
,

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}(d\gamma\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon})\right]\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon^{\beta}}\;.

It is straightforward to deduce the proof of Proposition
7.1

from the two propositions above. Therefore, the main goal of the next sections is to prove Propositions
7.2
and
7.3
. The proof
of Proposition
7.2
is given below whereas
the proof of Proposition
7.3

is much more involved and relies on several intermediate results detailed in the next sections.
Therefore, in this section we shall only prove Proposition
7.2
and conclude the proof of Proposition
2.11

based on Proposition
7.1
.

Proof of Proposition
7.2
.

We only provide the proof in the case when
x\in\mathcal{W}_{m}

as the proof when
x\in\mathcal{W}_{s}
is exactly similar. Let us
fix
x=(q,\,p)\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
throughout this proof. It follows
from the continuity of the trajectories of (
2.1
) that the
process
(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
starting
from
x\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
needs to cross the boundary
\partial\mathcal{W}

in order to reach
\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}
. As a result,

(7.4)

1-h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}}^{\epsilon}\leq\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})\;.

The stopping time
\zeta^{\epsilon}
defined in (
7.3
)
then satisfies
\zeta^{\epsilon}=\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}}^{\epsilon}\land\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}
under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
.
Moreover, applying Itô’s formula to the process
(V(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t)))_{t\geq 0}

at the time
\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t
ensures that,
\mathbb{P}_{x}
almost-surely, for
all
t\geq 0
,

V(q^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t),\,p^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t))-V(x)=-\gamma\int_{0}^{\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t}|p^{\epsilon}(r)|^{2}\mathrm{d}r+\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t}\langle p^{\epsilon}(r),\,\mathrm{d}B(r)\rangle+d\gamma\epsilon\left(\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t\right)\;.

As a result,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\left(\left(V(q^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t),\,p^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t))-V(x)\right)/\epsilon\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{\gamma}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t}|p^{\epsilon}(r)|^{2}\mathrm{d}r+\sqrt{2\frac{\gamma}{\epsilon}}\int_{0}^{\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t}\langle p^{\epsilon}(r),\,\mathrm{d}B(r)\rangle\right)\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma(\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t)}\right]\;.

It follows from the Girsanov argument developed in the proof of
[
28
, Lemma 3.8]

that

\mathcal{Z}:t\geq 0\mapsto\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{\gamma}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t}|p^{\epsilon}(r)|^{2}\mathrm{d}r+\sqrt{2\frac{\gamma}{\epsilon}}\int_{0}^{\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t}\langle p^{\epsilon}(r),\,\mathrm{d}B(r)\rangle\right)

is a martingale and under the probability measure
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}_{t}=\mathcal{Z}_{t}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}

the process

\left(B(r)-\sqrt{2\frac{\gamma}{\epsilon}}\int_{0}^{r}p^{\epsilon}(u)\mathrm{d}u\right)_{r\geq 0}

is a Brownian motion under the filtration
\sigma(B(r),\,r\in[0,\,t])
.
Therefore, letting the process

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{d}\widehat{q}^{\epsilon}(t)=\widehat{p}^{\epsilon}(t)\mathrm{d}t\;,\\
&\mathrm{d}\widehat{p}^{\epsilon}(t)=-\nabla U(\widehat{q}^{\epsilon}(t))\mathrm{d}t+\gamma\widehat{p}^{\epsilon}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\mathrm{d}B(t)\;,\end{aligned}\right.

and let
\widehat{\zeta}^{\epsilon}
be its first hitting time of
the set
\partial\mathcal{W}\cup\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
,
one deduces that for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\left(\ \frac{V(q^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t),\,p^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t))-V(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma(\widehat{\zeta}^{\epsilon}\land t)}\right]\;.

Additionally, computations done in
[
28
, Section 6.1]

ensure that the process
(\widehat{q}^{\epsilon}(t),\,-\widehat{p}^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}

and the process
(\widetilde{q}^{\epsilon}(t),\,\widetilde{p}^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}

defined in (
7.2
) share the same law, thus

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma(\widehat{\zeta}^{\epsilon}\land t)}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{(q,-p)}\left[\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma(\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon}\land t)}\right]\;,

where
\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon}
is defined in (
7.3
).
All in all this ensures that

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{V(q^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t),\,p^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}\land t))-V(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{(q,\,-p)}\left[\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma(\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon}\land t)}\right]\;.

As a result, taking
t\rightarrow\infty
by the dominated convergence
theorem in the left expectation and the monotone convergence theorem
in the right expectation yields

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{V(q^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}),\,p^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}))-V(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{(q,\,-p)}\left[\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon}}\right]\;.

Moreover, since

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{V(q^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}),\,p^{\epsilon}(\zeta^{\epsilon}))-V(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\right]\geq\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{V(\sigma,\,0)-V(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}}^{\epsilon}<\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\right).

One deduces that

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}}^{\epsilon}<\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\right)\leq\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{V(x)-V(\sigma,\,0)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathbb{E}_{(q,\,-p)}\left[\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma\tilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon}}\right]\;.

Inequality (
7.4
) then allows us to conclude
the proof of this lemma.
∎

Assuming Proposition
7.1
holds, let us now conclude this section
with the proof of Proposition
2.11
.

Proof of Proposition
2.11
.

Let us take a small constant
\delta>0
such that

(7.5)

V(\sigma,\,0)-\delta>\max(V(m,\,0),V(s,\,0))\;.

Then, let

\mathcal{A}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}:V(x)\leq V(\sigma,\,0)-\delta\}\;.

By (
2.14
),

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)\;.

Decompose the right hand side into

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{c}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)+\int_{\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{W}_{m}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)+\int_{\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{W}_{s}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)\;.

Also by Proposition
3.1
, there exists a constant
C>0

independent of
\epsilon
such that

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{c}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)\leq\frac{C}{Z_{\epsilon}}\mathrm{e}^{-(V(\sigma,\,0)-\delta)/\epsilon}=o_{\epsilon}(1)\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}(2\pi\epsilon)^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det{\mathbb{H}_{U}^{m}}}}e^{-V(m,\,0)/\epsilon}\;,

according to (
7.5
). Moreover, the first
bound in Proposition
7.1
ensures that

\int_{\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{W}_{m}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)=[1+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{W}_{m})=[1+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}(2\pi\epsilon)^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det{\mathbb{H}_{U}^{m}}}}e^{-V(m,\,0)/\epsilon}\;,

by the Laplace asymptotics on the function
\mathrm{e}^{-V(x)/\epsilon}

in
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{W}_{m}
.

Finally, the second bound in Proposition
7.1
ensures
the existence of constants
C,\,\beta>0
independent of
\epsilon

such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{W}_{s}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\epsilon^{\beta}}\int_{\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{W}_{s}}\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\mathrm{e}^{(V(x)-V(\sigma,\,0))/\epsilon}\mathrm{e}^{-V(x)/\epsilon}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C|\mathcal{A}|}{\epsilon^{\beta}}\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\mathrm{e}^{-V(\sigma,\,0)/\epsilon}=o_{\epsilon}(1)\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}(2\pi\epsilon)^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det{\mathbb{H}_{U}^{m}}}}e^{-V(m,\,0)/\epsilon}\;.

All in all, this guarantees that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)=[1+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}(2\pi\epsilon)^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det{\mathbb{H}_{U}^{m}}}}e^{-V(m,\,0)/\epsilon}\;,

which concludes the proof since
V(m,\,0)=U(m)
.
∎

8.
Transition density of the process absorbed at a boundary

Let us now delve into intermediate results aimed at proving Proposition
7.3
which proof is carried out in Section
10
. In this section, we provide an important identity involving the transition densities of the processes (
2.1
) and (
7.2
) absorbed when exiting the following bounded,
C^{2}
, open set

(8.1)

\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}:=\mathcal{W}\cap\overline{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{c}\cap\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{c}\;,

where
\mathcal{W}
is defined in (
7.1
). The proofs mainly follow the scheme developed in
[
28
, Sections 5 and 6]
.

Proposition 8.1
.

The process (
2.1
)
(resp. (
7.2
)) starting from
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}

and absorbed at the boundary
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
admits
a transition density
\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)

(resp.
\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}(x,\,y)
),
i.e. for all measurable set
A\subset\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
, for
all
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
and
t>0
,

(8.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}((q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))\in A,\,\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>t)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)\mathrm{d}y\,,

(8.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}((\widetilde{q}^{\epsilon}(t),\,\widetilde{p}^{\epsilon}(t))\in A,\,\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}^{\epsilon}>t)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}(x,\,y)\mathrm{d}y\;,

where
\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}
(resp.
\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}^{\epsilon}
)
is the first hitting time of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
for the process

(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
(resp.
(\widetilde{q}^{\epsilon}(t),\,\widetilde{p}^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
). Furthermore, for all
t>0
,
x,\,y\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
, it holds that

(8.4)

\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)=\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}(y,\,x)\;.

The proof of Proposition
8.1
is first obtained when the coefficients in (
2.1
)
and (
7.2
) are regularized. Recall then the sequence
(F_{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

defined in (
6.2
) and denote this time by
(q^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}

the process satisfying

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{d}q^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t)=p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}(t)\;,\\
&\mathrm{d}p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t)=F_{n}(q^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))\mathrm{d}t-\gamma p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\mathrm{d}B(t)\;.\end{aligned}\right.

and by
(\widetilde{q}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,\widetilde{p}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}

the process satisfying

(8.5)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{d}\widetilde{q}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t)=-\widetilde{p}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}(t)\;,\\
&\mathrm{d}\widetilde{p}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t)=-F_{n}(\widetilde{q}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))\mathrm{d}t+\gamma\widetilde{p}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\mathrm{d}B(t)\;.\end{aligned}\right.

Since
F_{n}
is a smooth function and the set
\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}

is open, bounded and
C^{2}
, the existence of the transition densities
for the processes
(q^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}

and
(\widetilde{q}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,\widetilde{p}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}

absorbed at the boundary
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
follows
from
[
15
, Chapter 14, Section 4]
.
We shall denote them by
\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)

and
\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)
.
Additionally, these transition densities vanish continuously on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times\partial(\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon})
.

Moreover, they satisfy the Kolmogorov backward and forward equation. Namely, if we define the differential operator

\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}=\langle p,\,\nabla_{q}\rangle+\langle F_{n}(q),\,\nabla_{p}\rangle-\gamma\langle p,\,\nabla_{p}\rangle+\alpha\epsilon\Delta_{q}+\gamma\epsilon\Delta_{p}\;,

with adjoint generator in the flat canonical space

(8.6)

(\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon})^{\dagger}=-\langle p,\,\nabla_{q}\rangle-\langle F_{n}(q),\,\nabla_{p}\rangle+\gamma\mathrm{div}(p\cdot)+\alpha\epsilon\Delta_{q}+\gamma\epsilon\Delta_{p}\;,

then, we have

(8.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon;\,x}\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\;y)\text{ and}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y))=\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon;\,y}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)\;,

where
\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon;\,z}
corresponds to the
differentiation with respect to the variable
z
.

Before proving Proposition
8.1
we show the following lemma obtained in
[
28
, Lemma 5.6]
for cylindrical domains. The proof is adapted here for the non-cylindrical survival set
\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon})
.
Then, for all
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
,

(8.8)

\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(y,\,x)g(y)\mathrm{d}y\underset{t\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}g(x)\;.

Proof of Lemma
8.2
.

For
t>0
, let us define the following covariance matrix

\Sigma_{t}:=\begin{pmatrix}2\alpha\epsilon t\,I_{d}&0\\
0&2\gamma\epsilon t\,I_{d}\end{pmatrix}\;.

Define the following transition density for
t>0
and
x,\,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,

\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y)=\frac{1}{(4\pi t\sqrt{\gamma\alpha\epsilon^{2}})^{d}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle x-y,\,\Sigma_{t}^{-1}(x-y)\rangle}\;.

If we replace the transition density in (
8.8
) by
\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y)
, the convergence follows by an appropriate change of variable. Therefore, we consider here the difference
\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)-\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y)
and provide a mild formulation of its expression which is then used to prove the convergence.

Let us fix
x,\,y\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
and let
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon})
with values in
[0,1]
such that
\varphi(x)=\varphi(y)=1
. Following the idea developed in
[
28
, Lemma 5.6]
, we define the following
function for
t>0
,

(8.9)

h_{t}:u\in(0,\,t)\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma u}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{u}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,z)\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,z)\varphi(z)\mathrm{d}z\;.

Step 1:
Let us then show that for
t>0
,

(8.10)

\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)-\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathrm{d}h_{t}(u)}{\mathrm{d}u}\mathrm{d}u\;.

It is sufficient to prove that

(8.11)

\lim_{u\rightarrow 0}h_{t}(u)=\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y),\quad\lim_{u\rightarrow t}h_{t}(u)=\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)\;.

Writing

h_{t}(u)=\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma u}\,\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,(\widetilde{q}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(u),\,\widetilde{p}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(u)))\varphi(\widetilde{q}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(u),\,\widetilde{p}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(u))\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}>u}\right]\;,

where
\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}

is the first hitting time of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
for
the process (
8.5
), the first limit then follows from the continuity of the trajectories of (
8.5
) and the transition density

\widehat{\mathrm{p}}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}
and from the condition
\varphi(x)=1
. Regarding the second limit, one can write

\displaystyle h_{t}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma u}\,\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{u}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\Bigl(x,\,y+\bigl(\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\,B(t-u),\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\,\widetilde{B}(t-u)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\times\varphi\Bigl(y+\bigl(\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\,B(t-u),\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\,\widetilde{B}(t-u)\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]\;.

where
(B(t))_{t\geq 0}
,
(\widetilde{B}(t))_{t\geq 0}
are independent Brownian motions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The proof of (
8.10
) then follows from the dominated convergence theorem, when
u\rightarrow t
, using the Aronson Gaussian upper-bound
[
2
]
satisfied by the transition density
\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{u}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}
.

Step 2:
Let
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon})
. Let us show the existence of a constant
C>0
such that for
t>0
,

(8.12)

\left|\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}g(x)\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}g(x)\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,x)\mathrm{d}x\right|\leq C\sqrt{t}\;.

Then, since

\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}g(x)\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,x)\mathrm{d}x=\mathbb{E}\left[g(y+(\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\,B(t),\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\,\widetilde{B}(t)))\right]\underset{t\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}g(y)\;,

the proof of (
8.8
) follows from the inequality (
8.12
).

Define now the following differential operator for
x=(q,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,

\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon;\,x}=\alpha\epsilon\Delta_{q}+\gamma\epsilon\Delta_{p}\;.

Notice that for all
t>0
,
x,\,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,

(8.13)

\partial_{t}\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y)=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon;\,x}\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y)=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon;\,y}\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y)\;.

Consequently, by (
8.7
), (
8.10
)
and (
8.13
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)-\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathrm{d}h_{t}(u)}{\mathrm{d}u}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma u}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}[\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon;\,z}(\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{u}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,z))\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,z)\varphi(z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\,-\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{u}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,z)\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon;\,z}(\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,z))\varphi(z)]\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}u\;.

By integration by parts and using the adjoint generator (
8.6
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)-\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma u}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}[\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{u}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,z)\left((\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon;\,z})^{\dagger}(\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,z))-\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon;\,z}(\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,z)\right)]\varphi(z)\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma u}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{u}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,z)[\langle\Sigma_{1}\nabla_{z}\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,z),\,\nabla\varphi(z)\rangle+\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,z)[(\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon;\,z})^{\dagger}-d\gamma]\varphi(z)]\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}u\;.

Furthermore, taking the gradient of
\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,\cdot)
ensures the existence of constants
C,\,\beta>0

such that for all
u\in(0,\,t)
,
y,\,z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,

\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,z)+|\nabla_{z}\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t-u}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(y,\,z)|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{t-u}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{|y-z|^{2}}{2\beta(t-u)}}}{(2\pi\beta(t-u))^{d}}\;.

By
[
2
]
, up to increasing the constants

C,\,\beta>0
, the density
\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{u}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}
also
admits a similar Gaussian upper-bound. Hence, we get

\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{u}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,z)\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{|x-z|^{2}}{2\beta u}}}{(2\pi\beta u)^{d}}\;.

Therefore, since
(\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon;\,z})^{\dagger}-\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,\epsilon;\,z}

is only composed of first order derivatives, the above Gaussian upper-bounds and the boundedness of the set
\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
provide a constant
C^{\prime}>0
which depends on
\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\lor\|\Delta\varphi\|_{\infty}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)-\widehat{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,\epsilon}(x,\,y)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma r}}{\sqrt{t-r}}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{|z-x|^{2}}{2\beta r}}}{(2\pi\beta r)^{d}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{|y-z|^{2}}{2\beta(t-r)}}}{(2\pi\beta(t-r))^{d}}\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\;C^{\prime}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{|y-x|^{2}}{2\beta t}}}{(2\pi\beta t)^{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma r}}{\sqrt{t-r}}\mathrm{d}r\;\leq\;2C^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\sqrt{t}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{|y-x|^{2}}{2\beta t}}}{(2\pi\beta t)^{d}}\;,

by the Chapman-Kolmogorov relation. Although the proof of the inequality above is done for fixed points
x,\,y\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
and the definition of
\varphi
depends on
x
and
y
. One can always do the exact same reasoning for any points
x,\,y
in a compact set
K\subset\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
which contains the support of the test function
g
in (
8.8
). Then, the same reasoning works for any points
x,\,y\in K
provided we take in (
8.9
) a test function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon})
equal to
1
on
K
. Thus, integrating the inequality above against the test function
g
concludes the proof of (
8.8
).
∎

Following the scheme of proof developed in
[
28
, Theorem 6.2]
, we conclude this section with the proof of Proposition
8.1
.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

Step 1:
Let us first prove the equality for the regularized processes: for all
t>0
,
x,\,y\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
,

(8.14)

\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)=\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(y,\,x)\;.

Let
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon})
and define for
t>0
and
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
,

u(t,\,x)=\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)g(y)\mathrm{d}y,\qquad v(t,\,x)=\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(y,\,x)g(y)\mathrm{d}y\;.

It is well known (see
[
13
, Chapter 2, Theorem 3.7]
)
that
u
is the unique solution in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon})\cap C_{b}(\mathbb{R_{+}}\times\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}})

of

(8.15)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}u(t,\,x)&=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\;n,\;\epsilon}u(t,\;x)&&t>0,\quad x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon},\\
u(0,\,x)&=g(x)&&x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon},\\
u(t,\,x)&=0&&t>0,\quad x\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\;,\end{aligned}\right.

Therefore, the equality (
8.14
) follows immediately if
v
is also a solution in
