C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon})\cap C_{b}(\mathbb{R_{+}}\times\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}})
of (
8.15
).

The equality
\partial_{t}v(t,\,x)=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}v(t,\,x)
follows from (
8.7
). Additionally, the continuity of
\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(y,\,x)

on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\times\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}

ensures that
v
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}

and vanishes at
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
. Finally, the continuity at
t=0
follows from Lemma
8.2
. Hence
v=u
and the equality (
8.14
) follows.

Step 2:
In order to deduce (
8.4
), we would like to take the limits
\alpha\rightarrow 0
,
n\rightarrow\infty
in (
8.14
). In order to do that, let us first prove that for all non-negative
f,g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon})
one has that for all
t>0
, for all
x=(q,p)\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
,
\mathbb{P}_{x}
almost-surely,

(8.16)

g(q^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}>t}\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0,\,n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}g(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>t}\;,

and

(8.17)

f(\widetilde{q}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\;\widetilde{p}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}>t}\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0,\,n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}f(\widetilde{q}^{\epsilon}(t),\,\widetilde{p}^{\epsilon}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}^{\epsilon}>t}\;.

Let us first control the distance between
(q^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}

and
(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
using Grönwall’s inequality. Let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
be an open bounded set of
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
such
that
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Let

\tau
be a positive and finite random variable. On the event
\{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}^{\epsilon}>\tau\}
,
there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\tau,\,\alpha,\,n

such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\,\tau]}|(q^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))-(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))|

(8.18)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\tau\sup_{q:(q,\,p)\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}|F_{n}(q)+\nabla U(q)|+\sqrt{2\alpha\epsilon}\sup_{s\in[0,\,\tau]}|B(s)|\right)\mathrm{e}^{C\tau}\;.

Notice that by (
6.3
), we have

\sup_{q:(q,\,p)\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}|F_{n}(q)+\nabla U(q)|\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

Using Grönwall’s inequality (
8.18
)
let us now prove that,
\mathbb{P}_{x}
almost-surely, for all
t>0
,

(8.19)

\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}>t}\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0,n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\penalty\ \mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>t}\;.

Notice that

(8.20)

\left|\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}>t}-\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>t}\right|\leq\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}>t,\,\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}<t}+\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}<t,\,\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>t}\;,

since the events
\{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}=t\}

and
\{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}=t\}

are of probability zero since the boundary
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}

has zero Lebesgue measure.

By
[
28
, Proposition 2.8]
, the process
(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
starting from
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
almost-surely never reaches the boundary
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}=\partial\mathcal{W}\cup\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}\cup\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}

with zero velocity. Therefore, by Lemma
4.1
, the process exits

\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
almost-surely immediately after hitting
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
and on the event
\{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}<t\}
, by the continuity of the trajectory of
(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, there exists
\delta>0
satisfying
\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}+\delta<\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}^{\epsilon}\land t
such that

(q^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}+\delta}^{\epsilon}),\,p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}+\delta))\notin\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}
. Taking

\alpha
small enough and
n\geq 1
large enough ensures by (
8.18
) that
(q^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}+\delta),\,p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}+\delta))\notin\overline{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}
,
which ensures that
\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}\leq\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}+\delta<t
. Regarding the second term in the right-hand side
of (
8.20
), on the event
\{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>t\}
, the process
(q^{\epsilon}(r),\,p^{\epsilon}(r))_{r\in[0,\,t]}
stays at a positive distance from
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
. Therefore, taking

\alpha
small and
n
large enough yields by (
8.18
)
that
\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}>t
as well. The convergence (
8.19
) then follows from the same argument as in Lemma
4.5
. Combining with Grönwall’s inequality (
8.18
), one obtains (
8.16
). The proof of (
8.17
) is exactly similar.

Step 3:
Let us now conclude the proof of Proposition
8.1
. By (
8.14
), one has for any non-negative
f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon})
,

(8.21)

\iint_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}f(x)\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)g(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\iint_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}f(x)\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(y,\,x)g(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\;.

Let us take the limit of the equality above when
\alpha\rightarrow 0
and
n\rightarrow\infty
. Notice first that the existence of the transition densities
\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)

and
\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}(x,\,y)

of the absorbed processes satisfying (
8.2
) and (
8.3
) is an immediate consequence of Radon-Nikodym’s theorem. Moreover, by the Fubini permutation and the convergence (
8.16
),

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}f(x)\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)g(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}f(x)\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[g(q^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}>t}\right]\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0,\,n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}f(x)\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[g(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>t}\right]\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}f(x)\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)g(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\;,

by definition of
\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)
. Furthermore, by (
8.17
),

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}f(x)\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(y,\,x)g(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}g(y)\mathbb{E}_{y}\left[f(\widetilde{q}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t),\,\widetilde{p}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}^{\alpha,\,n,\,\epsilon}>t}\right]\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha\rightarrow 0,\,n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}g(y)\mathbb{E}_{y}\left[f(\widetilde{q}^{\epsilon}(t),\,\widetilde{p}^{\epsilon}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}^{\epsilon}>t}\right]\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}f(x)\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}(y,\,x)g(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\;.

As a result, taking the limits
\alpha\rightarrow 0
,
n\rightarrow\infty

in (
8.21
) yields

(8.22)

\iint_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}f(x)\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\,y)g(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma t}\iint_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}f(x)\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}(y,\,x)g(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\;.

The equality (
8.4
) then follows from the local integrability of
\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D_{\epsilon}}}

and
\mathrm{p}_{t}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}\times\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
.
∎

9.
Lyapunov function and zero-noise Langevin process

Let
z\in\{m\}\cup\{s\}
be a local minimum of
U
. This section is dedicated to controlling, when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, the first exit time probability from a neighbourhood of
z
, and the distance between the underdamped Langevin process (
2.1
) and its zero-noise (
\epsilon=0
) counterpart. These results are essential for the proof of Proposition
7.3
carried out in the next section.

9.1.
First exit time probability

The proofs rely on the Lyapunov function
H
defined in
[
26
, Definition 4.1]
and translated in the
q
-coordinate as follows

(9.1)

H(x):=\frac{1}{2}|p|^{2}+\frac{\gamma-\lambda}{2}\left\langle q-z,\,p\right\rangle+\frac{(\gamma-\lambda)^{2}}{4}|q-z|^{2}+U(q)-U(z)\;,

where
\lambda>0
is a constant independent of
\epsilon
satisfying (
3.7
).

Lemma 9.1
.

There exist
\rho,\,\alpha>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that for
all
x\in\mathrm{B}(z,\,\rho)
,

(9.2)

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}H(x)\leq-\lambda H(x)+d\gamma\epsilon\;,

and

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}\bigg(\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\bigg)\leq\alpha d\gamma\,\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\;.

Proof.

Since
U
is a Morse function, its Hessian matrix at the local minimum

z
admits only positive eigenvalues. Therefore, the
asymptotic expansion of
U
in a neighbourhood of
z
ensures the existence of constants
\rho,\,c>0
such that for all
q\in\mathrm{B}(z,\,\rho)
,

\langle\nabla U(q),\,q-z\rangle\geq c(|q-z|^{2}+U(q)-U(z))\;.

Following the computation in
[
26
, Lemma 4.3]
, one deduces that for
x\in\mathrm{B}(z,\,\rho)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}H(x)

\displaystyle\leq-\lambda H(x)+\gamma\epsilon\,\Delta_{p}H(x)

(9.3)

\displaystyle\leq-\lambda H(x)+d\gamma\epsilon\;.

Consequently, for
\alpha>0
and
x\in\mathrm{B}(z,\,\rho)
,

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}\bigg(\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\bigg)=\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\left[\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}H(x)+\gamma\epsilon\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\left|p+\frac{\gamma-\lambda}{2}(q-z)\right|^{2}\right]\;.

Moreover, as in
[
26
, Lemma 4.2]
,
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,

\left|p+\frac{\gamma-\lambda}{2}(q-z)\right|^{2}\leq CH(x)\;.

As a result, by (
9.3
),

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}\bigg(\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\bigg)\leq\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\left[-\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}\lambda H(x)+\alpha d\gamma+C\gamma\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\epsilon}H(x)\right]\;.

Taking
\alpha\in(0,\lambda/C\gamma)
independent of
\epsilon
ensures that for
x\in\mathrm{B}(z,\,\rho)
,

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}\bigg(\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\bigg)\leq\alpha d\gamma\,\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\;,

thus concluding the proof.
∎

The Lyapunov control established in Lemma
9.1
allows to provide an upper-bound of the probability of exiting, before a time
t\geq 0
, a neighbourhood of
z
defined as follows

(9.4)

\mathcal{H}_{b}:=\{x\in\mathcal{W}_{z}:H(x)<b\}\;.

where
\mathcal{W}_{z}
is defined in Section
7
.

Proposition 9.2
.

There exist constants
\delta,\,\alpha,\,\beta,\,C>0

independent of
\epsilon
such that for all
0<a\leq b\leq\delta
,
for all
x\in\mathcal{H}_{a}
and
t\geq 0
,

(9.5)

\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}\leq t)\leq\frac{C(1+t)}{\epsilon^{\beta}}\mathrm{exp}\bigg(-\frac{\alpha(b-a)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\;.

Proof of Proposition
9.2
.

Let
\rho,\,\alpha>0
be as defined in Lemma
9.1
.
Since
H
is continuous, positive and vanishes only at
(z,\,0)

by
[
26
, Lemma 4.2]
, there exists
\delta>0
small enough
such that for all
b\in(0,\,\delta)
,

\mathcal{H}_{b}\subset\mathrm{B}(z,\,\rho)\;.

Step 1:
Let
a\in(0,\,b]
. We first prove the existence
of constants
\beta,\,C>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that for
all
t\geq 0
, for all
x\in\mathcal{H}_{a}
,

(9.6)

\mathbb{E}_{x}\bigg[\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))}{\epsilon}\bigg)\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}>t}\bigg]\leq C\left[1+\left(\frac{H(x)}{\epsilon}\right)^{\beta}\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\right]\;.

Let
x\in\mathcal{H}_{a}
. Apply Itô’s formula to the process

t\geq 0\mapsto\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}H\big(q^{\epsilon}(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}),\,p^{\epsilon}(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon})\big)\bigg)\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha d\gamma\big(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}\big)}\;.

From Lemma
9.1
, we deduce that for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}H\big(q^{\epsilon}(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}),\,p^{\epsilon}(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon})\big)\bigg)\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha d\gamma\big(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}\big)}\right]\leq\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\;,

ensuring that

(9.7)

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))}{\epsilon}\bigg)\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}>t}\right]\leq\mathrm{e}^{\alpha d\gamma t}\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\;.

Now let

T_{\epsilon}(x):=\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\left(\frac{\lambda H(x)}{d\gamma\epsilon}\right)\;.

By (
9.7
) there exist constants

C,\,\beta>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that for all
t\in[0,\,T_{\epsilon}(x)]
,

(9.8)

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))}{\epsilon}\bigg)\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}>t}\right]\leq C\left(\frac{H(x)}{\epsilon}\right)^{\beta}\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\;.

Assume now that
t\geq T_{\epsilon}(x)
, then by definition of
T_{\epsilon}(x)

one has

H(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}\leq\frac{d\gamma\epsilon}{\lambda}\;.

Therefore, following the proof of
[
26
, Proposition 4.9]

and using (
9.2
),
one deduces that for all
n\geq 0
and
t\geq T_{\epsilon}(x)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\left(\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}\right)^{n}H(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}>t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{n}\left(\frac{H(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}}{\epsilon}+\frac{d\gamma}{\lambda}\right)^{n}\leq\left(2\frac{\alpha d\gamma}{\lambda}\right)^{n}\;.

Consequently, for all
t\geq T_{\epsilon}(x)
,

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))}{\epsilon}\bigg)\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}>t}\right]\leq\mathrm{e}^{2\alpha d\gamma/\lambda}\;.

Combining with (
9.8
) concludes the proof
of (
9.6
).

Step 2:
Using the estimate (
9.6
)
let us now prove the inequality (
9.5
). For this purpose, we apply Itô’s formula to the process

t\geq 0\mapsto\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}H\big(q^{\epsilon}(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}),\,p^{\epsilon}(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon})\big)\bigg)\;.

By Lemma
9.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}H\big(q^{\epsilon}(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}),\,p^{\epsilon}(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon})\big)\bigg)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)+\alpha d\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}H\big(q^{\epsilon}(r),\,p^{\epsilon}(r)\big)\bigg)\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}>r}\right]\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)+Cd\alpha\gamma t\left[1+\left(\frac{H(x)}{\epsilon}\right)^{\beta}\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha H(x)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha a}{\epsilon}\bigg)+Cd\alpha\gamma t\left[1+\left(\frac{a}{\epsilon}\right)^{\beta}\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha a}{\epsilon}\bigg)\right]

since
x\in\mathcal{H}_{a}
. Moreover, since

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}H\big(q^{\epsilon}(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}),\,p^{\epsilon}(t\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon})\big)\bigg)\right]\geq\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\frac{\alpha b}{\epsilon}\bigg)\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}\leq t)\;,

one deduces that

\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}\leq t)\leq\mathrm{exp}\bigg(-\frac{\alpha(b-a)}{\epsilon}\bigg)+Cd\alpha\gamma t\left[\mathrm{exp}\bigg(-\frac{\alpha b}{\epsilon}\bigg)+\left(\frac{a}{\epsilon}\right)^{\beta}\mathrm{exp}\bigg(-\frac{\alpha(b-a)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\right]\;,

which concludes the proof of (
9.5
).
∎

9.2.
Distance to the zero-noise underdamped Langevin process

Let us now examine the distance, when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, between the underdamped Langevin process (
2.1
) and its zero-noise counterpart represented by

(9.9)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{d}q_{t}=p_{t}\mathrm{d}t,\\
&\mathrm{d}p_{t}=-\nabla U(q_{t})\mathrm{d}t-\gamma p_{t}\mathrm{d}t\;.\end{aligned}\right.

Recall below the notations
\mathcal{W}_{m}
and
\mathcal{W}_{s}
of Section
7
.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
K
be a compact subset of
\overline{\mathcal{W}_{z}}
such that
(\sigma,0)\notin K
. Then, for any
(q(0),\;p(0))\in K
,

(9.10)

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}(q(t),\,p(t))=(z,\,0)\;.

Furthermore, with
\rho
defined as in Lemma
9.1
,
there exist also constants
C,\,\lambda>0
such that for all
(q(0),\,p(0))

in
\mathrm{B}(z,\,\rho)
,

(9.11)

|(q(t),\,p(t))-(z,\,0)|\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}.

Proof.

We first prove the limit (
9.10
). Notice that for all
t\geq 0
,

(9.12)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(U(q(t))+\frac{1}{2}|p(t)|^{2}\right)=-\gamma|p(t)|^{2}\leq 0\;.

In particular, it entails that
q(t)
and
p(t)
are bounded independently of the time
t\geq 0
. And so are the time-derivatives below

(9.13)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}|p(t)|^{2}=2\langle p(t),\,-\gamma p(t)-\nabla U(q(t))\rangle,\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}U(q(t))=\langle p(t),\,\nabla U(q(t))\rangle\;.

Moreover, by (
9.12
),
U(q(t))+|p(t)|^{2}/2
is non-increasing in time and bounded
from below by
U(z)
. Therefore, it converges toward a limit
l\geq U(z)
. Assume that
l>U(z)
, then there exists a subsequence
of positive times
(t_{n})_{n\geq 0}
such that
t_{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty

and either

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}|p(t_{n})|^{2}>0\quad\text{or}\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}U(q(t_{n}))>U(z)\;.

Assume now that
c_{1}:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}|p(t_{n})|^{2}>0
.
Since the first time-derivative in (
9.13
) is
bounded then there exists a constant
\alpha>0
independent of
n

such that for all
n
sufficiently large and
t\in[t_{n}-\alpha,\,t_{n}]
,

(9.14)

|p(t)|^{2}\geq\frac{c_{1}}{2}\;.

Consequently, by (
9.12
), for

n
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle U(q(t_{n}))+\frac{1}{2}|p(t_{n})|^{2}

\displaystyle=U(q(t_{n}-\alpha))+\frac{1}{2}|p(t_{n}-\alpha)|^{2}-\gamma\int_{t_{n}-\alpha}^{t_{n}}|p(r)|^{2}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq U(q(t_{n}-\alpha))+\frac{1}{2}|p(t_{n}-\alpha)|^{2}-\frac{\gamma\alpha c_{1}}{2}\;.

Taking the limit
n\rightarrow\infty
above ensures that

l\leq l-\frac{\gamma\alpha c_{1}}{2}<l\;,

which is a contradiction, hence
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}|p(t)|^{2}=0
.
It remains therefore to analyze the case
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}U(q(t_{n}))>U(z)
.
Let us show that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\nabla U(q(t_{n}))=0

which shall ensure that necessarily
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}q(t_{n})=z
,
hence the proof of (
9.10
).

Assume that there exists a subsequence
(t_{n})_{n\geq 0}
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\nabla U(q(t_{n}))=w
where
w\neq\penalty\ 0
.
Then, using the boundedness of the time-derivative of
\nabla U(q(t))
,
since
U
is
C^{2}
, along with the convergence of
p(t_{n})

to zero, there exists
\alpha>0
such that for all
n
large enough
and
r\in[t_{n}-\alpha,\,t_{n}]
,

(9.15)

\left|\nabla U(q(r))-w\right|+\big|p(r)\big|\leq\frac{\alpha|w|}{2(1+\alpha+\gamma\alpha)}\;.

It follows from (
2.1
) that for all
n\geq 0
,

p(t_{n})+\alpha w=p(t_{n}-\alpha)-\gamma\int_{t_{n}-\alpha}^{t_{n}}p(r)\mathrm{d}r-\int_{t_{n}-\alpha}^{t_{n}}\left(\nabla U(q(r))-w\right)\mathrm{d}r\;.

Therefore, by (
9.15
),

\left|p(t_{n})+\alpha w\right|\leq\frac{\alpha|w|}{2(1+\alpha+\gamma\alpha)}(1+\alpha\gamma+\alpha)=\frac{\alpha|w|}{2}\;.

Then, taking
n\rightarrow\infty
ensures that

|\alpha w|\leq\frac{\alpha|w|}{2}\;,

which is a contradiction with the fact that
w\neq 0
, hence the proof
of (
9.10
).

Regarding the exponential control in (
9.11
),
its proof is identical to the proof of
[
26
, Theorem 2.3]

and follows from the Lyapunov function in (
9.2
).
∎

We conclude this section with a control of the distance between the trajectories of (
2.1
) and (
9.9
) in times of
order
1/\epsilon^{\lambda}
when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. The estimate presented below was obtained in
[
26
, Section 5]
and involves the
Gaussian process
(Y(t))_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
satisfying

(9.16)

\textup{d}Y(t)=\mathbb{A}(q(t))Y(t)\textup{d}t+\mathbb{J}\,\textup{d}\widehat{B}(t),\quad Y(0)=0\;,

where
(\widehat{B}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a
\mathbb{R}^{2d}-
Brownian motion and (see
[
26
, Equations 5.1, 5.3]
)

\mathbb{A}(q):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{O}_{d}&\mathbb{I}_{d}\\
-\mathbb{H}_{U}^{q}&-\gamma\mathbb{I}_{d}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathbb{J}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{O}_{d}&\mathbb{O}_{d}\\
\mathbb{O}_{d}&\mathbb{I}_{d}\end{pmatrix}\;.

Therefore,
Y(t)\sim\mathcal{N}_{d}(0,\,\Sigma_{t}(x))

where the covariance matrix
\Sigma_{t}(x)
is defined in
[
26
, Equation 6.2]
and depends on the initial
condition
x
of (
9.9
)
through the dependency of
\mathbb{A}(q(t))
in (
9.16
).
The following lemma is an immediate
consequence of
[
26
, Proposition 5.3 and Lemma 6.2]
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
\rho,\,b>0
be defined as in Lemma
9.1
and Proposition
9.2
. There exist constants
T>0
,

\lambda\in(0,\,1/4)
and
\beta>1
independent of
\epsilon
such
that for all
n\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C_{n}>0
such that
for all
t\in[T,\,1/\epsilon^{\lambda}]
, for all
x\in\mathrm{B}(z,\,\rho)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\bigg[\bigg|\left(q^{\epsilon}\big(t\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\big),\,p^{\epsilon}\big(t\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\big)\right)-\left(q\big(t\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\big),\,p\big(t\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\big)\right)-\sqrt{2\epsilon\gamma}Y\big(t\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\big)\bigg|^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{b}}^{\epsilon}>t\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C_{n}\epsilon^{n\beta}\;,

when
\epsilon>0
is small enough. Furthermore, there exist constants

C^{\prime},\,\alpha>0
and a symmetric positive definite matrix
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times 2d}

such that

(9.17)

\sup_{x\in\mathrm{B}(z,\,\rho)}\|\Sigma_{t}(x)-\Sigma\|_{F}\leq C^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\;.

10.
Proof of Proposition
7.3
.

In this section, we first analyze the trajectory of the process (
2.1
) starting from a point in
\mathcal{W}_{m}
and provide some preliminary results regarding the first exit time of a neighbourhood of the saddle point
(\sigma,0)
and the hitting time of
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
. These results are then used in a second subsection to prove Proposition
7.3
. The proofs rely in particular on results obtained in Sections
8
and
9
.

Remark 10.1
.

The statements shown in this section remain true if
\mathcal{W}_{m}

is replaced by
\mathcal{W}_{s}
as the proofs can be transposed identically to this case.

10.1.
Preliminary results

This first lemma allows us to control the probability of remaining in a small neighbourhood of
(\sigma,0)
. We define for
\rho>0
,

(10.1)

Q_{\rho}:=\overline{\mathcal{W}_{m}}\setminus\mathrm{B}(\sigma,\,\rho)\;.

Lemma 10.2
.

There exist constants
C,\,\rho>0
independent of
\epsilon

such that for all
t>0
,

(10.2)

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{W}_{m}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon}>t\big)\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{\epsilon t}}\;.

Proof of Lemma
10.2
.

It is sufficient to prove the inequality (
10.2
) with a supremum over
x\in\mathcal{W}_{m}\cap\mathrm{B}(\sigma,\,\rho)
since the probability vanishes on
Q_{\rho}
. Considering that
U
is a Morse function, its Hessian matrix at the saddle point

\sigma
admits one negative eigenvalue
-\lambda_{1}
and
d-1

positive eigenvalues
\lambda_{2},\dots,\lambda_{d}
. We denote by
(e_{1},\dots,e_{d})
the orthonormal basis associated with the eigenvalues
(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d})
and by
x_{i}=\langle x,\,e_{i}\rangle
the
orthogonal projection of the vector
x
on
e_{i}
.

Computing the asymptotic expansion of
U(q)
when
q
is close to
\sigma
one gets

U(q)=U(\sigma)-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}(q_{1}-\sigma_{1})^{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{d}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{2}(q_{i}-\sigma_{i})^{2}+O(|q-\sigma|^{3})\;.

As a result, if
U(q)<U(\sigma)
and
|q-\sigma|\leq\delta
for a sufficiently small
\delta
then there exists a constant

c_{1}>0
such that

(q_{1}-\sigma_{1})^{2}\geq c_{1}\sum_{i=2}^{d}(q_{i}-\sigma_{i})^{2}\;,

which ensures that
O(|q-\sigma|^{2})=O(|q_{1}-\sigma_{1}|^{2})
. Also,
up to changing the sign of
e_{1}
, one can assume that for

(q,\;p)\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
and
q\in\mathrm{B}(\sigma,\,\rho)
, then
q_{1}-\sigma_{1}>0
. Therefore, if we compute the asymptotics of
\langle\nabla U(q),\,e_{1}\rangle

for
(q,\;p)\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
and
q\in\mathrm{B}(\sigma,\,\rho)
,

\displaystyle\langle\nabla U(q),\,e_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbb{H}_{U}^{\sigma}(q-\sigma),\,e_{1}\rangle+O(|q-\sigma|^{2})

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{1}(q_{1}-\sigma_{1})+O((q_{1}-\sigma_{1})^{2})

\displaystyle\leq 0\;.

Furthermore, by (
2.1
),
it follows that

\mathrm{d}\left(q^{\epsilon}(t)+\frac{p^{\epsilon}(t)}{\gamma}\right)=-\frac{1}{\gamma}\nabla U(q^{\epsilon}(t))\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2\epsilon}{\gamma}}\mathrm{d}B(t)\;.

As a result, for all
t\geq 0
,

q^{\epsilon}(t)+\frac{p^{\epsilon}(t)}{\gamma}=q+\frac{p}{\gamma}-\frac{1}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{t}\nabla U(q^{\epsilon}(r))\mathrm{d}r+\sqrt{\frac{2\epsilon}{\gamma}}B(t)\;.

Therefore, for
(q,p)\in\mathcal{W}_{m}\cap\mathrm{B}(\sigma,\,\rho)
, on the event
\{\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon}>t\}
,

\big\langle q^{\epsilon}(t)+\frac{p^{\epsilon}(t)}{\gamma},\,e_{1}\big\rangle\geq\big\langle q+\frac{p}{\gamma},\,e_{1}\big\rangle+\sqrt{\frac{2\epsilon}{\gamma}}\langle B(t),\,e_{1}\rangle\;.

Since
\mathrm{B}(\sigma,\,\rho)
is bounded, there exists a constant

C>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that for
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon}>t)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\sqrt{\epsilon}|\langle B(t),\,e_{1}\rangle|\leq C)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(|\langle B(1),\,e_{1}\rangle|\leq C/\sqrt{\epsilon t})\leq\frac{2C}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon t}}\;,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Moreover, we can see that the time before entering a neighbourhood of
(m,0)
, for the zero-noise process (
9.9
), is a bounded function of the initial condition in
Q_{\rho}
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
\kappa>0
and define

(10.3)

\mathcal{S}:x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\mapsto\inf\{t\geq 0:U(q(t))+|p(t)|^{2}/2\leq U(m)+\kappa,\quad(q_{0},\,p_{0})=x\}\;.

Then, for
\rho>0
defined as in Lemma
10.2
,

(10.4)

\sup_{x\in Q_{\rho}}\mathcal{S}(x)<\infty\;.

Proof of Lemma
10.3
.

It follows from Lemma
9.3

that for all
x\in Q_{\rho}
,

\mathcal{S}(x)<\infty
. Therefore, if
\mathcal{S}
is also continuous on the set
Q_{\rho}
then the statement follows from the compactness of
Q_{\rho}
.

For
x\in Q_{\rho}
, we now denote by
(q(t;\,x),\,p(t;\,x))_{t\geq 0}
the trajectory
of (
9.9
) satisfying
(q(0),\,p(0))=x
.
Notice that, by the continuity of the trajectories of (
9.9
),

(10.5)

U(q(\mathcal{S}(x);\,x))+\frac{1}{2}|p(\mathcal{S}(x);\,x)|^{2}\leq U(m)+\kappa\;.

Moreover, for any
\theta>0
,

(10.6)

U(q(\mathcal{S}(x)+\theta;\,x))+\frac{1}{2}|p(\mathcal{S}(x)+\theta;\,x)|^{2}<U(m)+\kappa\;.

Indeed, if this is not the case then for all
t\in[\mathcal{S}(x),\,\mathcal{S}(x)+\theta)
,

(10.7)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(U(q(t;\,x))+\frac{1}{2}|p(t;\,x)|^{2}\right)=-\gamma|p(t;\,x)|^{2}=0\;.

Therefore, by (
9.9
), for
all
t\in(\mathcal{S}(x),\,\mathcal{S}(x)+\theta)
,

p(t;\,x)=p(\mathcal{S}(x);\,x)-\gamma\int_{\mathcal{S}(x)}^{t}p(r;\,x)\mathrm{d}r-\int_{\mathcal{S}(x)}^{t}\nabla U(q(r;\,x))\mathrm{d}r\;.

As a result,

\frac{1}{t-\mathcal{S}(x)}\int_{\mathcal{S}(x)}^{t}\nabla U(q(r;\,x))\mathrm{d}r=0\;.

Taking the limit when
t\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(x)
ensures that

\nabla U(q(\mathcal{S}(x);\,x))=0
which implies that
q(\mathcal{S}(x);\,x)=m
. Thus, since
p(\mathcal{S}(x);\,x)=0
,

U(q(\mathcal{S}(x);\,x))+\frac{1}{2}|p(\mathcal{S}(x);\,x)|^{2}=U(m)\;.

Therefore, we deduce from (
10.7
) that (
10.6
) is satisfied, hence a contradiction. As a result, (
10.6
) is satisfied for any
\theta>0
.

Notice that for
x\in Q_{\rho}
,
the trajectory
(q(t;\,x),\,p(t;\,x))_{t\geq 0}
is bounded in time since the energy
U(q(t;\,x))+|p(t;\,x)|^{2}/2

is a non-increasing function of time. Therefore, if we take
x,\;y\in Q_{\rho}
, since
U
is
C^{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, by Grönwall’s inequality there exists
C>0
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

\sup_{r\in[0,t]}|(q(r;\,x),\,p(r;\,x))-(q(r;\,y),\,p(r;\,y))|\leq\mathrm{e}^{Ct}|x-y|\;.

Therefore, if
\mathcal{S}(y)>0
, then for any
\alpha\in(0,\mathcal{S}(y))
, if
|x-y|
is small enough then

\inf_{t\in[0,\,\mathcal{S}(y)-\alpha]}U(q(t;\,x))+\frac{1}{2}|p(t;\,x)|^{2}>U(m)+\kappa,\quad U(q(\mathcal{S}(y)+\alpha;\,x))+\frac{1}{2}|p(\mathcal{S}(y)+\alpha;\,x)|^{2}<U(m)+\kappa\;,

which ensures that
\mathcal{S}(y)-\alpha<\mathcal{S}(x)<\mathcal{S}(y)+\alpha
. Similarly, if
\mathcal{S}(y)=0
, then
0\leq\mathcal{S}(x)<\alpha
. Hence the convergence
\mathcal{S}(x)\underset{x\rightarrow y}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{S}(y)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Using the result of Lemma
10.3
, we are now able to control the probability that the process (
2.1
) starting from
x\in Q_{\rho}
remains outside of a neighbourhood of
(m,0)
.

Lemma 10.4
.

For any
\delta>0
independent
of
\epsilon
, there exists
\theta=\theta(\delta)>0
such that for all
N\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C=C(N,\,\delta)>0
such that

\sup_{x\in Q_{\rho}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\,\delta)}^{\epsilon}\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}_{m}}^{\epsilon}>\theta\big)\leq C\epsilon^{N/2}\;,

where
\rho
is the radius defined in Lemma
10.2
.

Proof of Lemma
10.4
.

Fix
\delta>0
independent of
\epsilon
and
let us take
\kappa=\kappa(\delta)>0
small enough such that for all
x\in Q_{\rho}
,

U(q)+\frac{1}{2}|p|^{2}\leq U(m)+\kappa\,\,\Longrightarrow\,\,x=(q,\,p)\in\mathrm{B}(m,\,\delta/2)\;.

Then, by Lemma
10.3
, there exists
T=T(\delta)>0

such that for all initial conditions
(q(0),\;p(0))\in Q_{\rho}
,
the process (
9.9
) satisfies

(q(T),\,p(T))\in\mathrm{B}(m,\,\delta/2)\;.

Therefore, by the Markov inequality, for all
N\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\,\delta)}^{\epsilon}\land\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}_{m}}^{\epsilon}>T\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\sup_{t\in[0,\,T]}\big|(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))-(q(t),\,p(t))\big|>\delta/2,\,\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}_{m}}^{\epsilon}>T\big)

(10.8)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(\delta/2)^{N}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\bigg[\sup_{t\in[0,\,T]}\big|(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))-(q(t),\,p(t))\big|^{N}\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}_{m}}^{\epsilon}>T}\bigg]\;.

A standard application of the Grönwall inequality on the event
\{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}_{m}}^{\epsilon}>T\}
yields the existence
of a constant
C>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,\,T]}\big|(q^{\epsilon}(t),\,p^{\epsilon}(t))-(q(t),\,p(t))\big|\leq\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\,\mathrm{e}^{CT}\sup_{t\in[0,\,T]}|B(t)|\;.

Reinjecting into (
10.8
) concludes the proof.
∎

We conclude this subsection with the following lemma controlling the probability that the process (
2.1
) does not enter
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
in times of order
\log(1/\epsilon)
, when starting from a neighbourhood of
(m,0)
.

Lemma 10.5
.

There exist constants
c,\,\rho^{\prime},\,\theta^{\prime}>0

independent of
\epsilon
such that for
\epsilon
small enough,

\sup_{x\in\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>\theta^{\prime}\log(1/\epsilon))\leq 1-c\epsilon^{d}\;.

Proof of Lemma
10.5
.

Let us fix
\delta>0
small enough satisfying Proposition
9.2

and such that
\mathcal{H}_{\delta}\subset\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho)

where
\rho
is defined in Lemma
9.1
. Let
us take
T>0
large enough independent of
\epsilon
such that by (
9.11
)
in Lemma
9.3
, for any
(q(0),\,p(0))=x\in\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho)
,

(10.9)

\big|(q(T\log(1/\epsilon)),\,p(T\log(1/\epsilon)))-(m,0)\big|<\frac{\epsilon}{4}\;.

For
t\geq 0
, let
Y(t)
be the Gaussian vector satisfying (
9.16
) and define also

Z^{\epsilon}(t):=(q^{\epsilon}(t),p^{\epsilon}(t))-(q(t),p(t))-\sqrt{2\epsilon\gamma}\,Y(t)\,.

Consider now
x\in\mathcal{H}_{\delta/2}
and let us look at the following probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>T\log(1/\epsilon)\bigg)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq T\log(1/\epsilon)\bigg)

(10.10)

\displaystyle\leq 1-\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq T\log(1/\epsilon),\,\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\delta}}^{\epsilon}>T\log(1/\epsilon)\bigg)\;.

By Lemma
9.4
, up to increasing
T
,
we have the existence of
\beta>1
independent of
\epsilon
such
that for all
m\geq 1
there exists
C_{m}>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{H}_{\delta/2}
,

(10.11)

\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\big|Z^{\epsilon}(T\log(1/\epsilon))\big|^{m}\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\delta}}^{\epsilon}>T\log(1/\epsilon)}\big]\leq C_{m}\epsilon^{m\beta}\;.

Let us fix
m\geq 1
large enough such that
m(\beta-1)>d
. One has that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq T\log(1/\epsilon),\,\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\delta}}^{\epsilon}>T\log(1/\epsilon)\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg((q^{\epsilon}(T\log(1/\epsilon)),\,p^{\epsilon}(T\log(1/\epsilon)))\in\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\delta}}^{\epsilon}>T\log(1/\epsilon)\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\big|Y(T\log(1/\epsilon))\big|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4},\,\big|Z^{\epsilon}(T\log(1/\epsilon))\big|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2},\,\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\delta}}^{\epsilon}>T\log(1/\epsilon)\bigg)\;.

using the triangle inequality with (
10.9
). Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\leq T\log(1/\epsilon),\,\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\delta}}^{\epsilon}>T\log(1/\epsilon)\bigg)

(10.12)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\big|Y(T\log(1/\epsilon))\big|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}\bigg)-\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\delta}}^{\epsilon}\leq T\log(1/\epsilon)\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\big|Z^{\epsilon}(T\log(1/\epsilon))\big|>\frac{\epsilon}{2},\,\tau_{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\delta}}^{\epsilon}>T\log(1/\epsilon)\bigg)

(10.13)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\sqrt{2\gamma\epsilon}\big|Y(T\log(1/\epsilon))\big|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}\bigg)-C\frac{(1+T\log(1/\epsilon))}{\epsilon^{\beta}}\mathrm{exp}\bigg(-\frac{\alpha\delta}{2\epsilon}\bigg)-2^{m}C_{m}\epsilon^{m(\beta-1)}

by Proposition
9.2
and by applying the Markov inequality along with the inequality (
10.11
).
Notice that since we took
m
such that
m(\beta-1)>d
, the proof of the lemma follows if there exists a constant
c>0

independent of
x
and
\epsilon
such that for
\epsilon
small enough,

(10.14)

\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\big|Y(T\log(1/\epsilon))\big|\leq\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{4\sqrt{2\gamma}}\bigg)\geq c\epsilon^{d}\;.

For this reason, let us take a symmetric matrix
\mathbb{B}_{T\log(1/\epsilon)}(x)

such that
\mathbb{B}_{T\log(1/\epsilon)}^{2}(x)=\Sigma_{T\log(1/\epsilon)}(x)

where
\Sigma_{t}(x)
is the covariance matrix of
Y(t)
.
Then, taking a Gaussian vector
Z\sim\mathcal{N}_{2d}(0,\,\mathbb{I}_{2d})
,
it is easy to see that
Y(T\log(1/\epsilon))
and
\mathbb{B}_{T\log(1/\epsilon)}(x)Z

share the same law. Additionally,

\big|\mathbb{B}_{T\log(1/\epsilon)}(x)Z\big|\leq\sqrt{\big\|\Sigma_{T\log(1/\epsilon)}(x)\big\|_{\textup{F}}}|Z|\;,

since
\mathbb{B}_{T\log(1/\epsilon)}^{2}(x)=\Sigma_{T\log(1/\epsilon)}(x)
.
Therefore, by (
9.17
),
there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
x\in\mathcal{H}_{\delta/2}

such that

|\mathbb{B}_{T\log(1/\epsilon)}(x)Z|\leq C|Z|\;.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\big|Y(T\log(1/\epsilon))\big|\leq\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{4\sqrt{2\gamma}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\big|\mathbb{B}_{T\log(1/\epsilon)}(x)Z\big|\leq\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{4\sqrt{2\gamma}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\bigg(|Z|\leq\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{4C\sqrt{2\gamma}}\bigg)\;.

Let us define the constant
\beta=4C\sqrt{2\gamma}
. Since
|Z|\sim\chi(2d)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(|Z|\leq\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\beta}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\sqrt{\epsilon}/\beta}\frac{1}{2^{d-1}\Gamma(d)}x^{2d-1}\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{d-1}\Gamma(d)\beta^{2d}}\frac{\epsilon^{d}}{2d}\mathrm{e}^{-\epsilon/2\beta^{2}}+\frac{1}{2^{d-1}\Gamma(d)}\int_{0}^{\sqrt{\epsilon}/\beta}\frac{x^{2d+1}}{2d}\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq c\epsilon^{d}\;,

for
\epsilon
small enough, hence (
10.14
). Then, taking
\rho^{\prime}>0
small enough such that
\mathrm{B}(m,\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{H}_{\delta/2}
concludes the proof of Lemma
10.5
.
∎

10.2.
Proof of Proposition
7.3

Thanks to the lemmas obtained in the previous subsection, we are able to provide a sharp control on the
tails of the exit time
\zeta^{\epsilon}
defined in (
7.3
), which is then used to prove Proposition
7.3
.

Proposition 10.6
.

There exist constants

C,\,\alpha,\,\beta>0
independent of
\epsilon
, for
\epsilon
small enough, such that for all
t\geq 0
, for all
x\in\mathcal{W}_{m}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}
,

(10.15)

\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\zeta^{\epsilon}>t\big)\leq C\,\mathrm{exp}\big(-\alpha t\epsilon^{\beta}\big)\;,

where
\zeta^{\epsilon}
is defined in (
7.3
).

Proof of Proposition
10.6
.

Define the open set
\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}:=\mathcal{W}_{m}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}
. Starting from
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
, one has,
\mathbb{P}_{x}
almost-surely, the equality
\zeta^{\epsilon}=\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}
.

Step 1:
Let us now take a constant
m>2d+1
and let
T_{\epsilon}:=1/\epsilon^{m}
. We first prove the existence of a constant
c>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that for
\epsilon>0
small enough,

(10.16)

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>T_{\epsilon}\big)\leq 1-c\epsilon^{d}.

Let
Q_{\rho}
be the compact set defined in (
10.1
) where
\rho>0

is given by Lemma
10.2
. We first notice that for any
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
,

\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>T_{\epsilon}\big)\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>T_{\epsilon},\,\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon}<T_{\epsilon}/4\big)+\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon}\geq T_{\epsilon}/4\big)\;,

By Lemma
10.2
,
there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\epsilon
and
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}

such that

\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon}\geq T_{\epsilon}/4\big)\leq C\epsilon^{(m-1)/2}\;.

Furthermore, applying the strong Markov property at the stopping time

\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon}
,

(10.17)

\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>T_{\epsilon},\,\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon}<T_{\epsilon}/4\big)\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{\big(q^{\epsilon}(\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon}),\,p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{Q_{\rho}}^{\epsilon})\big)}\bigg(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>3T_{\epsilon}/4\bigg)\bigg]\;.

Taking
\rho^{\prime}>0
as defined in Lemma
10.5
, we deduce from Lemma
10.4
the existence of
a constant
C_{m}>0
such that for all
y\in Q_{\rho}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{y}\bigg(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>3T_{\epsilon}/4\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{y}\bigg(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>3T_{\epsilon}/4,\,\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho^{\prime})}^{\epsilon}<T_{\epsilon}/4\bigg)+\mathbb{P}_{y}\bigg(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{W}_{m}}^{\epsilon}\land\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho^{\prime})}^{\epsilon}\geq T_{\epsilon}/4\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{y}\bigg(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>3T_{\epsilon}/4,\,\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\;\rho^{\prime})}^{\epsilon}<T_{\epsilon}/4\bigg)+C_{m}\epsilon^{(m-1)/2}\;.

Moreover, applying the strong Markov property at the hitting time

\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho^{\prime})}^{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{y}\bigg(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>3T_{\epsilon}/4,\,\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho^{\prime})}^{\epsilon}<T_{\epsilon}/4\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho^{\prime})}^{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{\big(q^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho^{\prime})}^{\epsilon}),\,p^{\epsilon}(\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho^{\prime})}^{\epsilon})\big)}\bigg(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>T_{\epsilon}/2\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\tau_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>T_{\epsilon}/2\bigg)\;.

Therefore, by Lemma
10.5
and the definition of
T_{\epsilon}
,
there exists a constant
c\in(0,\,1)
independent of
\epsilon

such that for
\epsilon
small enough,

\mathbb{P}_{y}\bigg(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>3T_{\epsilon}/4,\,\tau_{\mathrm{B}(m,\,\rho^{\prime})}^{\epsilon}<T_{\epsilon}/4\bigg)\leq 1-c\epsilon^{d}\;.

Combining all these inequalities ensures the existence of a constant

C^{\prime}>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>T_{\epsilon}\big)\leq 1-c\epsilon^{d}+C^{\prime}\epsilon^{(m-1)/2}\;,

which concludes the proof of (
10.16
), for
\epsilon

small enough, since
(m-1)/2>d
.

Step 2:
Let us now deduce (
10.15
) using (
10.16
). For any
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
, by the Markov property,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>t\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\bigg(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>\left\lfloor\frac{t}{T_{\epsilon}}\right\rfloor T_{\epsilon}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq(1-c\epsilon^{d})^{\lfloor t/T_{\epsilon}\rfloor}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{exp}\bigg(\left\lfloor\frac{t}{T_{\epsilon}}\right\rfloor\log\left(1-c\epsilon^{d}\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{exp}\bigg(-ct\epsilon^{m+d}+c\epsilon^{d}\bigg)\;,

using the standard inequalities
\log(1-x)<-x
for
x\in[0,\,1)

and
\lfloor x\rfloor\geq x-1
for
x\geq 0
, hence (
10.15
).
∎

Finally, we conclude this section with the proof of Proposition
7.3
.

Proof of Proposition
7.3
.

We provide here the proof for the case
x\in\mathcal{W}_{m}
but as it was emphasized on Remark
10.1
all the results in this section can be extended to the case
x\in\mathcal{W}_{s}
using an identical proof.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}:=\mathcal{W}_{m}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}}
and recall the stopping times defined in (
7.3
). For
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
, one has that,
\mathbb{P}_{x}
almost-surely,

\zeta^{\epsilon}=\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon},\quad\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon}=\tilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\;,

Consequently, for all
x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\big(d\gamma\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon}\big)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\big(d\gamma\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}\big)\right]

\displaystyle=1+d\gamma\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\int_{0}^{\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}}\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma r}\right]\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=1+d\gamma\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma r}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq 1+d\gamma\epsilon\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma\epsilon}+d\gamma\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma r}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\widetilde{\tau}_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r)\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq 1+d\gamma\epsilon\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma\epsilon}+d\gamma\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma r}\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{r}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x,\;x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathrm{d}r\;,

by Proposition
8.1
. Additionally, by (
8.4
),

(10.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\big(d\gamma\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon}\big)\right]\leq 1+d\gamma\epsilon\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma\epsilon}+d\gamma\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}\mathrm{p}_{r}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x^{\prime},\,x)\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathrm{d}r\;.

Furthermore, by Chapman-Kolomogorov’s relation, for all
x,\,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}

and for all
r>\epsilon
,

(10.19)

\mathrm{p}_{r}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x^{\prime},\,x)=\mathbb{E}_{x^{\prime}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r-\epsilon}\mathrm{p}_{\epsilon}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}((q^{\epsilon}(r-\epsilon),\,p^{\epsilon}(r-\epsilon)),\,x)\right]\;.

Besides, using the Gaussian upper-bound shown in
[
28
, Theorem 2.19]

one has the existence of a constant
C_{1}>0
independent of
\epsilon

such that for all
x^{\prime\prime},\,x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
,

\mathrm{p}_{\epsilon}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x^{\prime\prime},\,x)\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{2d}\left(\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{12}\phi(\gamma\epsilon)\right)^{d}}}\;,

where
\phi
is a positive continuous function defined in
[
28
, Equation 74]

and satisfying
\phi(0)=1
. Therefore, there exists a constant
C_{2}>0

independent of
\epsilon
such that for all
\epsilon
small enough and for all
x^{\prime\prime},\,x\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
,

\mathrm{p}_{\epsilon}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x^{\prime\prime},\,x)\leq\frac{C_{2}}{\epsilon^{3d}}\;.

Reinjecting into (
10.19
)
ensures that for all
x,\,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
and for all

r>\epsilon
,

\mathrm{p}_{r}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x^{\prime},\,x)\leq\frac{C_{2}}{\epsilon^{3d}}\mathbb{P}_{x^{\prime}}(\tau_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon}>r-\epsilon)\;.

Moreover, by Proposition
10.6
,
there exist constants
C_{3},\,\alpha,\,\beta>0
independent of
\epsilon

such that for all
x,\,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}
, for all
r>\epsilon
,

\mathrm{p}_{r}^{\epsilon,\,\mathcal{D}_{\epsilon}}(x^{\prime},\,x)\leq\frac{C_{3}}{\epsilon^{3d}}\mathrm{exp}\big(-\alpha(r-\epsilon)\epsilon^{\beta}\big)\;.

Reinjecting
into (
10.18
) ensures the existence of a constant
C_{4}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathrm{exp}\big(d\gamma\widetilde{\zeta}^{\epsilon}\big)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1+d\gamma\epsilon\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma\epsilon}+\frac{C_{4}}{\epsilon^{3d}}\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\mathrm{exp}\big(-\alpha(r-\epsilon)\epsilon^{\beta}\big)\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=1+d\gamma\epsilon\mathrm{e}^{d\gamma\epsilon}+\frac{C_{4}}{\alpha\epsilon^{3d+\beta}}\;,

which concludes the proof of Proposition
7.3
.
∎

11.
Proof of Proposition
2.12

This section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
2.12
. The quantity
\mathrm{Cap}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})
therein corresponds to the capacity between the sets
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}
. The computation of its asymptotic when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
was done in the elliptic and reversible setting relying on the Dirichlet principle (see
[
9
,
8
]
) which is hardly tractable in non-reversible settings, see
[
23
]
.

In a recent work
[
25
]
, the authors devised a new approach for the elliptic and non-reversible setting which avoids the use of the Dirichlet principle. It relies on a weak definition of the capacity similar to the one provided in Proposition (
2.8
). By using appropriate test functions, the authors are then able to obtain the asymptotic of the capacity when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. However, their approach does not work in the current non-elliptic setting given that the computations therein rely on boundary regularity assumptions for the equilibrium potential function and also prior capacity bounds that are not available to us in the current non-elliptic setting. In this section, we adapt the reasoning in
[
25
]
in a way that cleverly avoids these difficulties.

For simplicity in this section we will assume
\sigma=0
and we shall
denote by
\mathbb{H}_{U}
(resp.
\mathbb{H}_{V}
) the Hessian
matrix of
U
(resp.
V
) at
\sigma
(resp. at
(\sigma,\,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
).
In particular, by definition of
V
(
2.4
), one has that

(11.1)

\mathbb{H}_{V}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{H}_{U}&\mathbb{O}_{d}\\
\mathbb{O}_{d}&\mathbb{I}_{d}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times 2d}\;.

For
x=(q,\,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, we shall use here the following
notation:

(11.2)

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d},\,x_{d+1},\;\ldots,x_{2d})=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{d},\,p_{1},\;\ldots,p_{d})

For
q,\,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we shall also denote by

C^{q,\,q^{\prime}}:=\left\{\phi\in C([0,\,1],\mathbb{R}^{2d}):\phi(0)=(q,\,0)\text{ and }\phi(1)=(q^{\prime},\,0)\right\}\;.

Remark 11.1
.

By Assumption
2.1
, one has that

\inf_{\phi\in C^{m,\,s}}\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}V(\phi(t))=U(\sigma)\;.

Besides, for any
\phi\in C^{m,\,s}
,

\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}V(\phi(t))=U(\sigma)\Longrightarrow\quad\exists t_{0}\in[0,\,1]\text{ such that }\phi(t_{0})=(\sigma,\,0)\;.

11.1.
Spectrum at the saddle point

Since
U
is a Morse function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{H}_{U}
admits one negative
eigenvalue
-\lambda_{1}<0
and positive eigenvalues
\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{d}>0
.
Therefore, by (
11.1
),
\mathbb{H}_{V}
admits
the following
2d
eigenvalues

-\lambda_{1},\,\lambda_{2},\ldots,\;\lambda_{d},\,\lambda_{d+1},\;\ldots,\lambda_{2d}\;,

where for all
i\in\llbracket d+1,\,2d\rrbracket
,
\lambda_{i}=1
.
We denote by
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2d})
an orthonormal diagonalisation
basis of
\mathbb{H}_{V}
.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let

(11.3)

\mathbb{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{O}_{d}&\mathbb{I}_{d}\\
-\mathbb{I}_{d}&\gamma\mathbb{I}_{d}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times 2d}\;.

Then, the matrix
\mathbb{H}_{V}\mathbb{M}
admits a unique negative
eigenvalue
-\mu
. Furthermore,

(11.4)

\mu=\frac{-\gamma+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+4\lambda_{1}}}{2}\;.

Besides, the eigenvalue
\mu
is associated to a unique eigenvector

v=(v_{q},\,v_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying

|v_{p}|=1
and
v_{q}=(\mu+\gamma)v_{p}
.

Proof.

Let
-\mu<0
be a negative eigenvalue of
\mathbb{H}_{V}\mathbb{M}

with eigenvector
v:=(v_{q},\,v_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
then

\mathbb{H}_{U}v_{p}=-\mu v_{q}=-\mu(\mu+\gamma)v_{p}\;.

In particular, this ensures that
v_{p}\neq 0
because
v=(v_{q},\,v_{p})\neq 0
.
Since
\mathbb{H}_{U}
admits a unique negative eigenvalue
-\lambda_{1}
,
one has that
\lambda_{1}=\mu(\mu+\gamma)
which ensures (
11.4
).
Moreover, since
v_{p}\neq 0
, we can rescale the eigenvector
v

such that it satisfies
|v_{p}|=1
.
∎

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
v
be the eigenvector defined in
Lemma
11.2
, then

-\frac{v_{1}^{2}}{\lambda_{1}}+\sum_{i=2}^{2d}\frac{v_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}}=-\frac{\gamma}{\mu}<0\;,

where
v_{i}=\langle v,\,e_{i}\rangle
.

Proof.

It is equivalent to showing that

-\langle v,\,\mathbb{H}_{V}^{-1}v\rangle=\frac{\gamma}{\mu}\;.

Moreover,

-\langle v,\,\mathbb{H}_{V}^{-1}v\rangle=-\langle v,\,\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{H}_{V}\mathbb{M})^{-1}v\rangle=\frac{1}{\mu}\langle v,\,\mathbb{M}v\rangle=\frac{\gamma}{\mu}|v_{p}|^{2}=\frac{\gamma}{\mu}\;.

∎

11.2.
Partitioning the phase space

In order to properly define the test function
f_{\epsilon}
used in (
2.16
) for the computation of the capacity
\mathrm{Cap}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})
,
we shall partition the phase space and describe the behaviour of
f_{\epsilon}
in each subspace.

First, let
K>0
be a large constant independent of
\epsilon
, which shall be precised later. Let also
\delta=\delta(\epsilon)=\sqrt{\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)}
and define the box
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}
centered around
(\sigma,\,0)

(11.5)

\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}:=\left[-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}\right]\times\prod_{i=2}^{2d}\left[-\frac{2K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}},\,\frac{2K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}\right]\;.

Let also

\partial\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}^{\pm}=\partial\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\left\{x_{1}=\pm\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}\right\}\;.

Lemma 11.4
.

For all
x\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\setminus(\partial\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}^{+}\cup\partial\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}^{-})

and for
\epsilon
small enough,

V(x)\geq U(\sigma)+\frac{5}{4}K^{2}\delta^{2}\;.

Proof.

By Taylor’s expansion, for all
x\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\setminus(\partial\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}^{+}\cup\partial\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}^{-})
,

\displaystyle V(x)

\displaystyle=U(\sigma)-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}|x_{1}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=2}^{2d}\lambda_{i}|x_{i}|^{2}+O(|x|^{3})

\displaystyle\geq U(\sigma)-\frac{1}{2}K^{2}\delta^{2}+2K^{2}\delta^{2}+O(\delta^{3})\;.

Therefore, for
\epsilon
small enough,

V(x)\geq U(\sigma)+\frac{5}{4}K^{2}\delta^{2}\;.

∎

Let us now consider the following set

\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}:V(x)<U(\sigma)+K^{2}\delta^{2}\}\;.

Let us also denote by
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{m}
(resp.
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{s}
)
the connected component of the set
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}

containing the point
(m,\,0)
(resp.
(s,\,0)
). The goal of this
subsection is to show that
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{m},\,\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{s}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
constitute a partition of
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
.

Analogously to (
11.5
), let us define the box below
for some
\beta>0
independent of
\epsilon
,

\mathcal{K}_{\beta}:=\left(-\frac{K\beta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\frac{K\beta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}\right)\times\prod_{i=2}^{2d}\left(-\frac{2K\beta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}},\,\frac{2K\beta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}\right)\;.

Notice that
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
for

\epsilon
small enough.

Lemma 11.5
.

For all
\beta>0
, there
exists a constant
c_{\beta}>0
such that for any
\phi\in C^{m,\,s}
satisfying
\phi(t)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
for all
t\in[0,\,1]
,
then

(11.6)

\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}V(\phi(t))>U(\sigma)+c_{\beta}\;.

Proof.

If the assertion above is not true then by covering the bounded domain
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{\beta}

with balls of radius
1/n
and drawing a polynomial trajectory between
them one can build a set of
M
-Lipschitz continuous functions
\phi_{n}\in C^{m,\,s}
,
for some
M>0
independent of
n\geq 1
, such that for all
n\geq 1
,

\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}V(\phi_{n}(t))\leq U(\sigma)+\frac{1}{n}

and for all
t\in[0,\,1]
,
\phi_{n}(t)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
.
Moreover, the
\phi_{n}
are bounded independently of
n
since
they are all
M
-Lipschitz continuous on
[0,\,1]
. As a result,
by Arzela-Ascoli’s theorem, up to taking an appropriate subsequence, one has the existence of
\phi\in C^{m,\,s}

such that

\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}|\phi_{n}(t)-\phi(t)|\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\;.

In particular, this ensures that for all
t\in[0,\,1]
,
\phi(t)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
.
Also, since
V
is
C^{1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and the functions

\phi_{n}
are uniformly bounded on
[0,\,1]
, one has the existence of
a constant
C>0
independent of
n\geq 1
such that for all
t\in[0,\,1]
,

|V(\phi_{n}(t))-V(\phi(t))|\leq C\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}|\phi_{n}(t)-\phi(t)|\;.

As a result, for all
t\in[0,\,1]
,

\displaystyle V(\phi(t))

\displaystyle\leq|V(\phi(t))-V(\phi_{n}(t))|+\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}V(\phi_{n}(t))

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}|\phi_{n}(t)-\phi(t)|+U(\sigma)+\frac{1}{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}U(\sigma)\;.

Therefore,
\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}V(\phi(t))\leq U(\sigma)
. By Remark
11.1
,
necessarily,

\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}V(\phi(t))=U(\sigma)\;.

Also, by Remark
11.1
, the path

\phi
necessarily attains
(\sigma,\,0)
at a certain time which
is in contradiction with the fact that for all
t\in[0,\,1]
,
\phi(t)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
.
Hence the validity of Lemma
11.5
.
∎

In order to prove that
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{m},\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{s}

and
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
constitute a partition of
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
,
we first show the following disjointness result.

Lemma 11.6
.

For
\epsilon
sufficiently small,
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{m}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{s}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Assume that there exists
x\in\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{m}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{s}

then one can construct a path
\phi\in C^{m,\,s}
with
values in
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}
. We fix
\beta>0
independent of
\epsilon
. Necessarily
\phi
enters
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
, otherwise, by
Lemma
11.5
, there exists a
constant
c_{\beta}>0
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,\,1]}V(\phi(t))>U(\sigma)+c_{\beta}\;,

which leads to a contradiction by taking
\epsilon
small enough such that
K^{2}\delta^{2}<c_{\beta}
.

As a result,
\phi
enters the set
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}

at a time
T_{1}>0
such that for all
t\leq T_{1}
,
\phi(t)\in\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{m}
.
Following the same computation done in Lemma
11.4
for
\beta
small, necessarily
\phi
enters
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}

through its boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{\beta}\cap\{x_{1}=\pm K\beta/\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}\}

since the values of
\phi
are assumed to be in
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
.
Therefore, up to changing the sign of
e_{1}
we can assume that

\phi
enters through the boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{\beta}\cap\{x_{1}=K\beta/\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}\}
.
Furthermore,
\phi
never exits the set
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
through
its boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{\beta}\cap\{x_{1}=-K\beta/\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}\}
.
Otherwise, using Lemma
11.4
again, necessarily
\phi
enters
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}

which is in contradiction with the definition of
\phi
.

Let

T_{2}:=\inf\{T\in[0,\,1]:\phi(t)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\beta}\text{ for all }t\in(T,\,1]\}\;.

We define
g
as a continuous path on
[0,T_{2}]
with values in
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
such that
g(0)=(m,\,0)
and
g(T_{2})=\phi(T_{2})
. Let us now define

\displaystyle\psi(t)=\begin{cases}g(t),&t\in[0,T_{2}]\\
\phi(t),&t\in[T_{2},1]\;.\end{cases}

Then,
\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{m,\,s}
and by construction,

\psi(t)\notin\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, which leads to a contradiction by Lemma
11.5
for
\epsilon
sufficiently small.
∎

Lemma 11.7
.

For
\epsilon
sufficiently small, the
sets
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{m}
,
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{s}

and
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
constitute
a partition of
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The proof is now immediate from Lemma
11.6
.
∎

11.3.
Definition of the test function

The idea of the proof of Proposition
2.12
is to consider
a suitable test function
f_{\epsilon}
in (
2.16
) for the computation of the
capacity
\mathrm{Cap}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})
. The test function
f_{\epsilon}
is such that
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}f_{\epsilon}\simeq 0
on the neighbourhood

\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
of the saddle
point
(\sigma,\,0)
. The capacity
\mathrm{Cap}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})

is then shown to behave in its highest order as an integral over the
boundary
\partial\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}
for which we are able to
compute the asymptotic when
\epsilon
goes to zero.

When
x=(q,\,p)\in\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
,
the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
defined in (
2.2
)
behaves in the first order as the following operator:

(11.7)

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}=\langle p,\,\nabla_{q}\rangle-\langle\mathbb{H}_{U}q,\,\nabla_{p}\rangle-\gamma\langle p,\,\nabla_{p}\rangle+\gamma\epsilon\Delta_{p}\;.

Therefore, we consider the function

(11.8)

j_{\epsilon}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{2\pi\gamma\epsilon}}\int_{-\infty}^{\langle x,\,v\rangle}\exp\left(-\frac{\mu}{2\gamma\epsilon}t^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}t\;,

where
\mu
is defined in Lemma
11.2
. An easy computation shows that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,

(11.9)

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}j_{\epsilon}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{2\pi\gamma\epsilon}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mu}{2\gamma\epsilon}\langle x,\,v\rangle^{2}\right)\left[-\langle\mathbb{H}_{V}\mathbb{M}v,\,x\rangle-\mu\langle x,\,v\rangle\right]=0\;.

The idea is then to define the test function
f_{\epsilon}=j_{\epsilon}

on
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
and extend
it smoothly on
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
such that
f_{\epsilon}=1

on
\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
and
f_{\epsilon}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}

using the partitioning of Lemma
11.7
. In order to do that, let us take
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\,[0,\,1])

satisfying

\displaystyle\varphi(t)=\begin{cases}0,&t\leq 0\\
1,&t\geq 1\;.\end{cases}

In particular, the smoothness of
\varphi
in
\mathbb{R}
implies
that for all
n\geq 1
,

(11.10)

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}t^{n}}(0)=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}t^{n}}(1)=0\;.

For
\theta>0
, we shall denote by
\varphi_{\theta}
the function
defined for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
as follows:

(11.11)

\varphi_{\theta}(x)=\varphi\left(\frac{|x_{1}|-K\delta/\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}{\theta}\right)\;.

Furthermore, in order to extend the function
f_{\epsilon}
smoothly
outside of
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
, we shall need the following lemma.

Lemma 11.8
.

There exists a smooth function
g_{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d},\,[0,\,1])

satisfying

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(x)=\begin{cases}1,&V(x)\leq U(\sigma)+\frac{K^{2}\delta^{2}}{2}\\
0,&V(x)\geq U(\sigma)+K^{2}\delta^{2}\;.\end{cases}

Besides, there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\epsilon

such that

(11.12)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(|\nabla g_{\epsilon}(x)|+|\nabla^{2}g_{\epsilon}(x)|\right)\leq\frac{C}{\delta^{6}}\;.

Proof.

Let
\Psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d},\,\mathbb{R})
be a non-negative
function with compact support in
\mathrm{B}(0,\,1)
and integrating
to
1
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, let us define

(11.13)

g_{\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\delta^{6d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\mathbf{1}_{V(x-y)\leq U(\sigma)+2K^{2}\delta^{2}/3}\Psi(y/\delta^{3})\mathrm{d}y\;.

As a mollified function it is clear that
g_{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d},\,[0,\,1])
.
Also, using the change of variable
z=(x-y)/\delta^{3}
and using the fact that

\Psi
admits a compact support, one easily shows the derivative
controls satisfied by
g_{\epsilon}
in (
11.12
).

Additionally, using Assumption
2.3
and following the proof of Proposition
3.1
,
one shows that
V(x)\underset{|x|\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty
.
In particular, there exists
M>0
such that for all
|x|\geq M
,

V(x)>U(\sigma)+2K^{2}\delta^{2}/3
. Therefore, for
\epsilon
small
enough, if
|x|\geq M+1
and
|y|\leq\delta^{3}
, then

V(x-y)>U(\sigma)+2K^{2}\delta^{2}/3\;,

hence
g_{\epsilon}(x)=0
for
|x|\geq M+1
since
\Psi
has support in the ball
\mathrm{B}(0,\,1)
.
Moreover, since
V
is
C^{1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
it
is
C
-Lipschitz continuous on the ball
\mathrm{B}(0,\,M+2)
for
some constant
C>0
.

Let us now take
x\in\mathrm{B}(0,\,M+1)
such that
V(x)\leq U(\sigma)+\frac{K^{2}\delta^{2}}{2}
.
Then for
\epsilon
small enough and
|y|\leq\delta^{3}
one has
that

\displaystyle V(x-y)

\displaystyle\leq|V(x-y)-V(x)|+V(x)

\displaystyle\leq C\delta^{3}+U(\sigma)+\frac{K^{2}\delta^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\leq U(\sigma)+\frac{2K^{2}\delta^{2}}{3}\;.

Therefore, reinjecting into (
11.13
) ensures that

g_{\epsilon}(x)=1
. Consider now the case
V(x)\geq U(\sigma)+K^{2}\delta^{2}
.
Then similarly,

\displaystyle V(x-y)

\displaystyle\geq V(x)-|V(x-y)-V(x)|

\displaystyle\geq U(\sigma)+K^{2}\delta^{2}-C\delta^{3}

\displaystyle>U(\sigma)+\frac{2K^{2}\delta^{2}}{3}

for
\epsilon
small enough, hence
g_{\epsilon}(x)=0
which concludes
the proof.
∎

Let us conclude this subsection with the expression of the test function

f_{\epsilon}
. In order to do that let us define the boxes

\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{m}=\left[\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}+\theta\right]\times\prod_{i=2}^{2d}\left[-\frac{2K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}},\,\frac{2K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}\right]

and

\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{s}=\left[-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}-\theta,\,-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}\right]\times\prod_{i=2}^{2d}\left[-\frac{2K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}},\,\frac{2K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}\right]\;.

Now let

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon,\,\theta}(x)=\begin{cases}g_{\epsilon}(x)\left[(1-\varphi_{\theta}(x))j_{\epsilon}(x)+\varphi_{\theta}(x)\right],&x\in\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon,\theta}^{m}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}\\
g_{\epsilon}(x)j_{\epsilon}(x),&x\in\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}\\
g_{\epsilon}(x)(1-\varphi_{\theta}(x))j_{\epsilon}(x),&x\in\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon,\theta}^{s}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}\\
1,&x\in\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{m}\\
0,&x\in\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}^{s}\cup(\mathbb{R}^{2d}\setminus\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon})\;.\end{cases}

Lemma 11.9
.

The function
f_{\epsilon,\,\theta}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{c})

and satisfies
f_{\epsilon,\,\theta}(x)=1
for
x\in\partial\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The smoothness of
f_{\epsilon,\,\theta}
follows from the partitioning
of
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}
in Lemma
11.7
, the
vanishing of the derivatives of
\varphi_{\theta}
using (
11.10
)
and the smoothness of
g_{\epsilon}
defined in Lemma
11.8
.
∎

11.4.
Computation of
\mathrm{Cap}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})

We show here that the capacity
\mathrm{Cap}_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon})

in (
2.16
) can be computed
by choosing the test function
f_{\epsilon,\,\theta}
and taking the limit of the integral when
\theta\rightarrow 0
. The rest of this section is thus devoted to the proof of the theorem below which directly implies Proposition
2.12
.

Let

(11.14)

\alpha_{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\frac{(2\pi\epsilon)^{d}}{2\pi}\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{|\det{\mathbb{H}_{U}}|}}e^{-U(\sigma)/\epsilon}\;,

where
\mu
is defined in Lemma
11.2
.

Theorem 11.10
.

\lim_{\theta\rightarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}(x)(-\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}f_{\epsilon,\,\theta}(x))\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x=[1+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\alpha_{\epsilon}\;.

The proof of this theorem relies on the following propositions.

Proposition 11.11
.

\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}|\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}j_{\epsilon}(x)|\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x=o_{\epsilon}(1)\alpha_{\epsilon}\;.

For any vector
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2d})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, let us
denote the vector

(11.15)

\widetilde{x}=(x_{2},\ldots,x_{2d})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d-1}

such that for any
i\in\llbracket 1,\,2d-1\rrbracket
,
\widetilde{x}_{i}=x_{i+1}
.
Also, let

(11.16)

\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{\pm}:=\left\{\widetilde{x}\in\prod_{i=2}^{2d}\left[-\frac{2K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}},\,\frac{2K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}\right]:V\left(\pm\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)<U(\sigma)+K^{2}\delta^{2}\right\}\;.

Proposition 11.12
.

One has that

(11.17)

\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\int_{\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{+}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)(-\widetilde{x}_{d})\left(1-j_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\right)\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{V\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{x}=[1+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\alpha_{\epsilon}\;.

Also,

(11.18)

\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\int_{\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{-}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}\left(-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\widetilde{x}_{d}\,j_{\epsilon}\left(-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{V\left(-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{x}=o_{\epsilon}(1)\alpha_{\epsilon}\;.

Let us now prove Theorem
11.10
.

Proof.

First notice that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
such that
|x_{1}|>K\delta/\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}
,
one has that

1-\varphi_{\theta}(x)\underset{\theta\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\;,

by definition of
\varphi_{\theta}
in (
11.11
).
Therefore, by definition of
f_{\epsilon,\,\theta}
, one has that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}(x)(-\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}f_{\epsilon,\,\theta}(x))\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}(x)(-\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(g_{\epsilon}j_{\epsilon}))(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x}_{=I_{\epsilon}^{1}}\;+\;\underbrace{\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{m}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}(x)(-\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}\varphi_{\theta}(x))(1-j_{\epsilon}(x))g_{\epsilon}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x}_{=I_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{s}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}(x)\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}\varphi_{\theta}(x)j_{\epsilon}(x)g_{\epsilon}(x)\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x}_{=I_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{3}}\;+\;o_{\theta}(1)\;.

Let us first prove that

(11.19)

I_{\epsilon}^{1}=o_{\epsilon}(1)\alpha_{\epsilon}\;.

Notice that by the definition (
11.8
),
j_{\epsilon}(x)\in[0,\,1]

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. Moreover, there exists a constant

C_{1}>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\nabla j_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\;.

Additionally, by Lemma
11.8
,
g_{\epsilon}(x)\in[0,\,1]
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}

and there exists a constant
C_{2}>0
independent of
\epsilon

such that

(11.20)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(|\nabla g_{\epsilon}(x)|+|\nabla^{2}g_{\epsilon}(x)|\right)\leq\frac{C_{2}}{\delta^{6}}\;.

Besides, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(g_{\epsilon}j_{\epsilon})(x)=g_{\epsilon}(x)\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}j_{\epsilon}(x)+j_{\epsilon}(x)\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}g_{\epsilon}(x)+2\gamma\epsilon\langle\nabla_{p}j_{\epsilon}(x),\,\nabla_{p}g_{\epsilon}(x)\rangle\;.

As a result, since
h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}(x)\in[0,\,1]

by (
2.14
),

|I_{\epsilon}^{1}|\leq\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}|\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}j_{\epsilon}(x)|\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}|\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}g_{\epsilon}(x)|\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x+2\gamma C_{1}\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}|\nabla g_{\epsilon}(x)|\mu_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}x\;.

Proposition
11.11
ensures that the first
integral in the right-hand side of the inequality above is of order

o_{\epsilon}(1)\alpha_{\epsilon}
. By Lemma
11.8
,
\nabla g_{\epsilon}(x)=0

whenever
V(x)\leq U(\sigma)+K^{2}\delta^{2}/2
. Moreover, when
V(x)>U(\sigma)+K^{2}\delta^{2}/2
,

\mathrm{e}^{-V(x)/\epsilon}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-U(\sigma)/\epsilon}\epsilon^{K^{2}/2}\,,

since
\delta=\sqrt{\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)}
. Thus, using the gradient control (
11.20
) and taking the constant
K
large enough and independent of
\epsilon
, we deduce (
11.19
).

Let us now show that

(11.21)

\lim_{\theta\rightarrow 0}I_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{2}=[1+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\alpha_{\epsilon}\;.

By definition of
\varphi_{\theta}
in (
11.11
)
one has that

I_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{2}=\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{m}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\;\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}(x)\frac{-x_{d+1}}{\theta}\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\frac{x_{1}-K\delta/\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}{\theta}\right)(1-j_{\epsilon}(x))g_{\epsilon}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-V(x)/\epsilon}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}x_{2d}\;.

Let us consider only the integral with respect to the first variable

x_{1}
and let us perform the change of variable
t=(x_{1}-K\delta/\sqrt{\lambda_{1}})/\theta
.
Thus, we obtain using the notation (
11.15
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}+t\theta,\,\widetilde{x}\right)(-\widetilde{x}_{d})\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}t}(t)\left(1-j_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}+t\theta,\,\widetilde{x}\right)\right)g_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}+t\theta,\,\widetilde{x}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}+t\theta,\,\widetilde{x}\right)/\epsilon}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)(-\widetilde{x}_{d})\left(1-j_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\right)g_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)/\epsilon}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}t}(t)\mathrm{d}t+o_{\theta}(1)\;.

Therefore, since

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}t}(t)\mathrm{d}t=\varphi(1)-\varphi(0)=1\;,

one has using the notation (
11.16
),

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\rightarrow 0}I_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{+}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)(-\widetilde{x}_{d})\left(1-j_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\right)\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{V\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{x}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{+}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)(-\widetilde{x}_{d})\left(1-j_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\right)\left(1-g_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\right)\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{V\left(\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{x}\;.

Using Proposition
11.12
, it remains
to show that the second integral above is of order
o_{\epsilon}(1)\alpha_{\epsilon}
.
By definition of
g_{\epsilon}
in Lemma
11.8
,
if
1-g_{\epsilon}(x)\neq 0
then
V(x)\geq U(\sigma)+K^{2}\delta^{2}/2
.
Therefore, taking again
K
large enough ensures that the second integral is of order
o_{\epsilon}(1)\alpha_{\epsilon}
which yields (
11.21
).

Finally, it remains to show that

(11.22)

\lim_{\theta\rightarrow 0}I_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{3}=o_{\epsilon}(1)\alpha_{\epsilon}\;.

Similarly to the computation of
I_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{2}
, one has that

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\rightarrow 0}I_{\epsilon,\,\theta}^{3}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{-}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}\left(-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\widetilde{x}_{d}\,j_{\epsilon}\left(-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)g_{\epsilon}\left(-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{V\left(-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Q}_{\epsilon}^{-}}h_{\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon},\,\mathcal{S}_{\epsilon}}^{*}\left(-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\widetilde{x}_{d}\,j_{\epsilon}\left(-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{V\left(-\frac{K\delta}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},\,\widetilde{x}\right)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{x}+o_{\epsilon}(1)\alpha_{\epsilon}\;.

Proposition
11.12
then concludes
the proof of Theorem
11.10
.
∎

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Propositions
11.11

and
11.12
. Let us start with
Proposition
11.11
which relies on the following
lemma.

Lemma 11.13
.

The matrix
\mathbb{H}_{V}+\frac{\mu}{\gamma}v\otimes v^{\dagger}

admits one zero eigenvalue of dimension 1 and all other eigenvalues
are positive.

Proof.

The proof is exactly similar to the proof of
[
25
, Lemma 8.2]

and mostly relies on Lemma
11.3
.
∎

Let us move on now to the proof of Proposition
11.11
which is similar to the proof of
[
25
, Proposition 8.5]
.

Proof of Proposition
11.11
.

By (
11.9
), for any
x\in\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}j_{\epsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{2\pi\gamma\epsilon}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mu}{2\gamma\epsilon}\langle x,\,v\rangle^{2}\right)\langle\nabla U(q)-\mathbb{H}_{U}q,\,v_{p}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mu}{2\gamma\epsilon}\langle x,\,v\rangle^{2}\right)O_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}|\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}j_{\epsilon}(x)|\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mu}{2\gamma\epsilon}\langle x,\,v\rangle^{2}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-V(x)/\epsilon}\mathrm{d}x\,O_{\epsilon}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-U(\sigma)/\epsilon}\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\left\langle\left(\mathbb{H}_{V}+\frac{\mu}{\gamma}v\otimes v^{\dagger}\right)x,\;x\right\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}x\,O_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

By Lemma
11.13
, let
\rho_{1}=0
and
\rho_{2},\ldots,\rho_{2d}>0

be the eigenvalues of
\mathbb{H}_{V}+\frac{\mu}{\gamma}v\otimes v^{\dagger}
.
Therefore, there exists
M>0
independent of
\epsilon

such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\cap\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}}|\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}j_{\epsilon}(x)|\mu_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\mathrm{e}^{-U(\sigma)/\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d-1}}\int_{-M\delta}^{M\delta}\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\sum_{k=2}^{2d}\rho_{k}y_{k}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}y_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}y_{2d}\,O_{\epsilon}(1)
